-3-
These are the main causes of the defeat. Generally
speaking the Japanese public, who have not been very
politically minded, expected too much from the Socialists,
because they proclaimed thet they were friends of the
poor masses.  However in reality the party gave them
high prices of commodities, rigid and ever-lasting controlled
economy and unreasonable rates of taxes to the great
disappointment of the masses. Thus the party was in a
dilenma between the people's c. 1 'nts and the responsi-
bility to practice occupation policies. The revision of
the civil service law initiated by General Headquarters
made the situation worse. If the party had considered
the selfish interest of the party first,it should have
left the government immediately after the appearance of
SCAP's letter on this subject. If it had acted so smartly,
i could not have given excuses for Communists to attack
the Socialists. But the staff of the porty maintained a
loyal attitude to the Occupation Authol.ty end remained
in office.
The Victory of the Democratic Liberals
We attribute the biggest cause of the Democratic
Liberals' over-whelming victory to Yoshida's personal
character. He received nttion-wide applause because of
his errogant attitude towards the Occupation Forces. A
coc rative attitude towards the Occupation Forces is
becoming quite unpopular among the Japanese people.

On the Gain of Communists
1. The gain of the Communists from 4 to 35 seats
in the House of Representatives overshadows the future
of democracy in this country. The majority being held
by the conserv:tives will not bring security in protect-
ing Japan from the totalitarian menace, unless some
drastic measures ere taken by the Allied Authority to
combat this new phase in the Diet. It is almost useless to
say that the Conmunists are seeking to find an opportunity
of overthrowing the Government by violence. In such a
case the conservatives will not hesitate to appeal to
violence too. In order to check such extremists, the
Social Democrats must stand in between. The Socialists
are the only pover which can fight against the communist
idealogy, not by violence but by thought. It is the only
party which can struggle on the same platform with the
Communists in trade-unions and fermers' unions. Naturally,
the advancement of the Co munists at the saorifice of the
Socialists means more than the . v of the Derocratic
Liberals. Indeed, it jeopardizes true democracy in
Japan as advocated by the Occupation Forces.
2. a) Just like the Japanese supported Yoshida'
stiffness to the Allied Forces, they applauded the Anti-
American struggle of the Comlunists, which a ppealel to
nationalistic sentiments. The main slogan of the Reds
was "Slave of Foreign Capitalism or Independence of the
Nation". It attributed the difficulties of living to
ScAP's policy explaining the crowded traffic by the export
of locomotives and treins and the shortage of textile
commodities by that of every inch of textile.
b) It claimed thet Japan is being comreiled to
commodities at high prices from abroad and export cem-
modities at low prices It continued saying that it
proved the slavish adherence of the Govern ent of Japan
to Foreign Towers, thet the Japanese were foreed to cst suger
only for the time being
c) Against this irrosponsible and felse pror
made by thle Conmunists, the Soeialists had to suy taet we
are a defeated nation and ust not forget the coed will of
the American people. The Socialists slso must tell the
people thet it was agoinst the true spirit of dexoev ey
to insist
"ted claims. However, such preashings
were unpopular for collecting votes.
d) Literary activities are being carried out by
the Communists on a large se le, while that of the
Socialists is incredibly roor.The Corm unists have their
own daily paper and a number of periodicels are being
edited by Conmunists or their fellow travelers. :ost of
the union bulletins are under Com unist influence
Since

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.98)
Edolosure co Tokyo's
Despatah No. 146,
Maroh 12,1949.
-5-
Since Communist influence is so great in the literary
field that writers and critics are not only afraid to
criticize Communists, but also do not forget to praise
them. On the contrary, these writers make very possible
effort to make the Socialist Party unpopular among the
people. There is a strong Communist influence, though
indirect, in the Paper Allocation Committee. The Socialists
tried to obtain at least the same amount of paper for
their own propaganda as that allotted to the Communist
orgens. But they were told that the door is entirely
closed for a new applicstion.
3. While Socialists were working hard in the Diet,
Government, and otler public assemblies, the Communists
made a close relation with the people by taking up the
peoples' complaints. Communists established consulting
offices at least one in each wardor town where they took
care of the reople in reducing the amount of taxes xed
by tax offices, or took housewives en mass to the ward
offices demanding special rations of rice. They sometimes
threatened landlords in regard to house problems. They
entertain children with paper puppets, teach social dances
to the young folks, welcome repatriated soldiers at ports
and stations. These dail:
ities attracted poor
people, of low intelligence. It must be stressed that the
future of Communism is being cultivated while the low
intelligence of the masses furnishes hot beds for rovolution
in the future. Under such circumstances their strength
will increase ore and more.
4. Communists seem to have abundent funds to cerry
out such daily activities as well as political campaigns.
For instance, they pay fabulous prices in order to establish
t. consultin offices. Conmunist organizers are
usually being well paid. The Socialists must learn such
elaborate and constant activities of the Conmunists, but
they do not know how to raise the necessary funds.
5. The Communists have succeeded in inviting some
intellectual people to their camp, asking them to cooperate
with the party. The Socialists have also many promihent
friends, but they are not well organized to secure their
full cooperation for the party. This  ust be done
immediately.
6. The Communists aie siving strong influence to
unions. While so far the Jocialists respected the initietive
of trade unions ey id not make any close relation with
them. But hereafter the party  ust make itself more
closely affiliated with the nions.

Despatch No. 146,
March 12, 1949.
-6-
7. The victory of the Communists in China brought
many benefits to the Communists here. Labor leaders and
some intellectuals were threatened by the Communists
here who said to them that those who opposed the Communists
would be hanged sooner or later, because Japan would come
under the influence of the Reds quite soon. Such threats
often adopted by the Communists for various purposes must
not be used by the Socialists.
CONCLUSION
The retreat of the midile-road policy was unfortunate
for the purpose of peace and democracy. However, democracy
cannot be cultivated artif:
The defeat of the
Socialists will give them a chance to reflect. It is
questionable just how any people voted with definite
intention. The Mainichi published its own study made
January 26-27, asking people whether they ex ect sometling
out of this new political phase. Tifty percent enswered
no or thet they have little expectation, twenty-one per-
cent said cannot tell and only twenty-seven percent
answered satisfying the result.  ig from this study
so meny people voted without much thinking. This fact
is a great disappointaent to those who tried to democratize
the Japanese people. The Socialist Party must pay its
best effort for its reconstruction as well as for the
democratization of the Japanese people. Should it feil,
the country will be over run either by reactionaries or
totalitarianism.

1. The writer of this report served in Japen first during late 1945 es

B. Conelusions:
The breakdown of prioe control and rationing in Japan from the
very beginning heve so aoted that two million government workers
have been driven into the arms of the Communist Party.
Marxism has had a long tradition in Japen. Under Generals Araki
and Doihara, et al, the Young frtcers and onlistedmen of the
Japenese Army during the 1931-l943 period were earefully sehooled
in economio dootrines thet combined Marxism with Tego's and Arski's
expansionism. The Communist Party of post-wer Japan has utilized
this Marxist tredition in its maroh to power. GHQ has developed
no effe etive counter-propagende program.
The NCIU (Netional Congress of Industrial Unions) end some of 1ts
satellite organizations are not trade unions but revolutionary syn-
dioates. Util zing the Fer Eastern Commission "Sixtreen Prineiples
for Japanese Trade Uniona" as a fecade, the NCIU unions have built
a fine propaganda campaign throughout Japan that is anti-demooratie
and anti-Occupation. The NCIU unions, or certain parts such as
the Youth Seotions, should be dissolved by SCiß order es exhihiting
fesoist and totaliterian trends.

The Future of the Democratic Party
Since Mr. Kojina considers a split in the party
inevitable, his remarks on the future course of the
Democratic Party are confined to the Ashida faction,
which he hopes will retain the name "Democretic Party".
Mr. Kojime is anong those in the party who believe thet
the Democrats shoula remain cutsile the coalition, He
feels that the Democrats should concentrate on building
up their local party organization. cooperate with the
Socialists as much as possible within the Diet, and seek
to prevent a situation where the Socialists, as an
opposition party, will be drawn toward the connunists.
A very interesting diseussion ensued as to the tactics
of building up local party organization and Mr. Kojima
was interested in various techniques used in the United
States and England. Kr. Kojima thinks that if the entire
Democratic Party joins the coalition then in the next
election both the Democratic and the Democratic Liberal
Party will lose votes and these votes will go in part
to the Socialists, but more important the protest votes
will also strengthen the position of communis ts. On the
other hand, if the Demoerats remain outside the coalition,
the protest votes against the Democratie Liberal Party
in the next election will go in large pert to the Democrats
and Socialists with the communists receiving a smaller
pereentage of this vote

Mr. Kojima personally f vors  course of action

now gone over to Inukai. Mr. Kojima thinks it is now
possible for the Demooratic Party to cooperate more closely
with the Socialists. He considers the eventual amalgamation
of the Ashida faction and the right-wing Socialists
desirable, but he admits that this is not possible in
the immediate future. Meanwhile, he considers it better
for both the Demoorats and the Socialists to remain outside
the government.

Subject: The Social Democratic Party's Shift to the Left.

I have the honor to bring to the Department's
attention the reorganization of the Social Democratic
Party at 1ts fourth national convention (March 15-17,
1949), as a result of which left wing elements have
secured virtual control of the party.
Ir. sUZUKI Mosaburo, leftist leader, was elected
Seoretary General in & substantial victory over
Mr. ASANUNA Inajiro of the right wing. For the first
time sinoe the organization of the party at the end
of the war a leftist has secured the secretary general-
ship. A new thirty-ran Central Committee has been chosen,
comprising ten right-wingers, ten left-wingers, five
neutrals, and five meabers representing the leftist
Japan Liaison Counoil of Labor Unlons (Zenroren), thus
giving the leftist a potentially preponderant pogition.
Mr. KaTaYAin Tetau was reelected party Chair an as a
compromise gesture to the right wing, but he is expected
to be over-shadowed by the more aggressive Secretary
General. The convention also voted to amend the party's
constitution to provide for e communist-style politburo
of five members, local chepters at workshop levels,
and public eleotion of the Central Executive Committee
by party members.
The results of the party's reorganization indicate
a marked inerease in the influence of labor union leaders,
including Demooratization League elements of the National
Congrese of Industrial Unions. Because of the Social
Demoorutic Party's support of the National Public Service
Law amendmant in 1948, the Leugue initially intended to
organize a labor party of its own. More recently, however,
it deoided to concentrete its attention in effecting the
reconstruction of the Sooial Demooratic Party along more
leftist lines.

CONFIDENTIAL
The reconstruction of the Social Democratic Party
at the fourth national convention has thus given labor
a far stronger position in the party than it ever enjoyed
before. It is likely, therefore, that the party will
become more truly a labor party, although at the cost
of moving further to the left.
The national convention rejected the left wing's
insistence upon a "scientific socialisn", or Marxist
plank, in the party's platform. Instead, the party
has committed itself to the attainment of an equally
vague program of "social democracy" through the acquisi-
tion of power in the National Diet. To accomplish this
end, however, the convention adopted certain communist
techniques, such as closer identification with the mass
labor movement and what is virtually the cell principle
of organization at the lowest or so-called workshop
level. The party has in this way moved much closer
toward becoming a class party, clthough it has still
drawn a line against the class principles of the communists.
While the Social Demooratic Party has moved much
further toward the left, it is still considerably to the
right of the Japan Communist Party. Mr. Suzuki, the
new Seoretary General, is a thorough leftist, but of
the legal type. He has apparently drawn a certain limita-
tion beyond which he will not venture, as illustrated
by his remaining within the party last fall vwhen ultre-
leftist elements of the Kuroda faotion withdrew to form
the communist-like Labor-Farmer Party because of dis-
satisfaotion with the conservstive leadership of the
Sooialists. Like Mr. KaTo Kanju, Mr. Suzuki is not a
Marxist, and he has been as opposed to suggested mergers
with the Japan Communist Party as any of his party's
right wingers. While noted for his intellectual integrity
and with some reputation as an economic expert, Mr. Suzuki
has given little evidence of being a brilliant or particu-
larly capable political leader. His theories of socialism
laok olarity" As a politiciun, he betrays considerable
confusion in his thinking. He is anti-capitelistic
without sharing the comiunists' anti-capitalism. In
the confused middle ground between right and left, between
oapitalism and comaunism, Mr. Suzuki might well lose his
way. Desplte his anti-comcunist views, he could be easily
susceptible to a comaunist-inspired popular front movement
if it were offered in the right garb.
In moving further to the left, the Social Denocratic
Party has undoubtedly strengthened itself. It will now
be able to bid more successfully for mass labor support.
In this sense, the party may be able to counter more
effectively
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokyo'suespatch No.24
Apr11 21, 1949.
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
effectively postwar communist inroads into the labor
movement, a consideration which was apparently behind
the decision of the anti-communist Demooratization
League to throw its support to the Social Demoorats
rather than attempt to create a new, "reformed" socialist
or labor, party of its own. On the other hand, the
reorganization of the Social Democratic Party will most
likely sever its connection with the more liberal elements
of the Democratic Party, the basis upon which the coalition
Katayama and Ashida cabinets were built.
The national convention of the Socialists has by
no means erased the heterogeneous, factional character
of the Social Democratic Party. Each step in the convention's
deliberations, from the election of officers to the
definitions of party policy, was marked by compromises
and adjustments between the left and right wings. But
in this process of comcromise and adjustment the center
of gravity has definitely shifted to the left, and, while
an open split between the two extremes has been evoided,
the party is still a coalition of conflicting factions.
The inoreased strength of the party's left wing
can be attrlbuted only in part to labor's larger role
in party affairs as a result of the national convention.
Equally Important is the decline in strength and prestige
of the right wing leaders. From the time the party was
organized at the cnd of the war under right wing auspices,
the rightists have been unable to provide asgressive,
dynamio loadership. Men like Katayama and Asanuma
apparently lack the political acumen end forceful
personality strong leadership demands. In s sense, most
of the Sooiul Democratio Party's leaders ore too far ahead
of their time. Japanese politics, for Il the postwar
changes, is still oharacterized by cortuin traditional,
so-oalled "feudal" aspects of leadership in which the
oya-bun, or boss, depends upon a follo iny based upon
Strong personal loyalties. Too any of the Socialist
leaders luck such strong personal followings end have
depended upon the more impersonel support of the intangible
eleotorate. At party conforenco tables and in the rough-
and-tumblo of intra-puruy strife a Jealer's personal
popularity ut the polls ls too often of less weight
than the numbor und influonce of his personal backers.
The sociul Democratio Party leader who was perhaps
best uelifled from the standpoint of the size and
strength of his personul following was Mr. EIRano Rikizo.
This former right-wins leeder, however, was disqualified
under the purge, appurently at the inatigation primarily
of General Headquarters. His disqualification deprived
the purty's right wine of its strongest and perhaps
moot able louder.

General of the Army Douglas MacArthur,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
Dear General:
On March 28, 1949 a Japanese Diet delegation
visited the Soviet Mission and handed a cony of a
petition adiressed to you in the name oi numerous
trade unions, cultural" organizations and various
political parties requesting to cancel the decision
of the Purge Committee regarding disqualification of
Jiichiro Matsumoto, former Vice-Prosident of the House
of Councillors.
Having scrutinized this petition and other pertinent
materials, I came to the conclusion thst the decision
of the Purge Committee regarding Mn Liatsumote was
absolutely unjust and incorrect. Juch a decision
might be explained by nothing else but an aspiravion
of the antidemocratic cireles of Japan to renove from
the political activities tlose politicians, who are
undesirable for them.
Mr. Jiichiro Matsumoto is one of the politicians
who has been fighting iere than 30 years for emaneipation
of the outcast "TA" caste. Jince the surrenler of Japan
he has been an active adherent of the democratization
of his country end occupied such posts as the Vice-
President of the Association for Freedom and Human Rights,
the Chairan of the Japan Labor-Farmer Relief Association,
the Chairman of the "Suiheisha" an others, end thus he
has conciliated the confidence and favour of the wide
Japanese ublic.
This may be proved by the indignation, that has
spread now among the Japanese public in connection with
the decision regarding Mr. Matsumoto's politicel disquali-
fication.
Such

=== Page 233 === (confidence 0.97)
April 21, 1949.
-2-
Such a decision is an evident example of how the
Purge Committee is used in order to oppress demooratie
lealers. At the same time there is a number of cases
showing how this Committee has been used in order to
defend supporters of the Japanese militarists.
In connection with this I would like to remind
a few names of those persons who had been rehabilitated
by the Purge Committee without any foundation for
such action:
Ken Inukai - one of the ex-leaders of the so-called
Asia Development Committee, the former Economic Adviser
of Wang Ching-wei's puppet government.
Wataru Narahashi - one of the supporters of the
Japanese militarists, who was very active in China.
Tatsuo Morita - former leader of the so-called
patriotic movement in industry, the author of a number
of militaristic books as well as of the "German Labor
Front"
I hope that you will take neasures to cancel the
incorrect decision regerding Mr. Matsumoto's political
disqualification.
Sincerely yours,
/s/ K. Derevyanko

sUBJECT: Tokyo's 242 of Apri1 21, 1949
the deteriaas
palitical situa
At its fourth national convention, the left-wing of the
Socialist Party gained virtual control of the party. A leftisto 
first time in the party's history. The leftists obtained a
potentially preponderant position on the Central Committee and
voted an amendment to the party's constitution, providing for a
Communist-style politburo of five members, local chapters at
workshop levels and popular election of the Central Executive
Committee. By adopting certain techniques such as closer iden-
tification with the mass labor movements and what is virtually
the cell principle of organization at the workshop level, the
Socialist party moved closer toward becoming a class party, al-
though it is far from espousing Communist principles.
In moving left-ward, the Socialist Party is in a better
Q501649
nosition to bid for mass labor supnort which it lost to a large
extent as a result of having supported the National Public Serv-
ice Law amendments on labor. In this sense, the party may
counter more effectively postwar Communist inroads into the
labor movement, a consideration which evidently caused the
Democratization League subsequently to back up the Socialists
rather than to form a new "Labor Party". The leftist trend of
the Socialists, on the other hand, will almost inevitably block
any fusion between the Socialist Party and the liberal elements
of the Democratic Party.
CS/Y
As for leadership, Katayama was reelected party Chairman
as a compromise gesture to the right wing. He is expected to
be overshadowed by Suzuki who, though a confirmed leftist, is
not a Marxist and has been opposed to suggested mergers with
the JCP. However, he is not particularly capable. His
theories lack clarity. He could, despite his anti-Communist
118
views "be easily suscentible to a communist-inspired popular
front movement if it were offered in the right garb". The
Confidentia
ablest and best qualified leader of the Socialist Party is
Hirano Rikizo, former right-wing leader. He was disqualified
under the purge, apparently at the instigation of General
Headquarters.
FE should see. An aporaisal sheet is attached.

I have the honor to bring to the Department's

1949), as a result of which left wing elements have
secured virtual control of the party
G
Ir. SUZUKI Mosaburo, leftist leader, Was elected
Secretary General in a substantial victory over
Mr. ASANUNA Inajiro of the right wing. For the first
time since the oranization of the farty ut tre end
of the war a leftist has secured the seeretery general-
ship. A new thirty- n Central Conmittee has been chosen,
comprising ten right-wingers, ten left-wingers, ive
neutrals, und five moabers representing the lertist
Japan Liaison Council of Labor Unions (Zenroren), thus
giving the leftist a potentially preponderant position.
Mr. KaTaYAi Tetsu was reelected party Chair an as a
compromise gesture to the right wing. but he is expected
to be over-shadowed by the more asgressive Secretary
General. The convention also voted to amend the party's
constitution to provide for a communist-style politburo
of five members, loeal chspters at vorkshop levels,
and public election of the Central Executive Committee
by party members
The results of the party's reorganization iudicate
a marked inerease in the influenee of labor unien leaders,
including Democratization League elements of the National
Congress of Industrial Unions Because of the Social
Democratie Party's support of the National Public Service
Law amendment in 1948, the Len jue initially intended to
organize a Jabor party of its own. More recently, however,
it decided to concentrate its attention in effecting the
reconstruction of the Social Democratic Party along more
leftist lines.

The reconstruction of the Social Democratic Party
at the fourth ne tional convention has thus given labor
ε far stronger position in the party than it ever enjoyed
before. It is likely, therefore, thet the party will
become more truly a labor party, although at the cost
of moving further to the left.
The national convention rejected the left wing's
insistence upon a "scientific socialism", or Marxist
plank, in the party's platform. Instead, the party
has committed itself to the attain ent of an equally
vague program of "sociel de ocracy" through the acquisi-
tion of power in the National Diet. To accomplish this
end, however, the convention adopted certain communist
technigues, such as closer identificction with the mass
labor movetent and vh t is virtually the cell principle
of organ ization at the lowest or so-culled workshop
level. The party has in this way toved nuch closer
toward becoming a class party, clthough it has still
drawn a line against the class principles of the communists.
While the Social Demoeratic Party has moved much
further toward the left, it is still considerably to the
right of the Japan Communist Party. Lir. Suzuki, the
new Secretary General, is a thorough leftist, but of
the legal type. He has apparently drawn a certain limita-
tion beyond which he will not venture, as illustrated
by his remaining within the party last fall when ultra-
leftist elements of the Kuroda faction withdrew to form
the communist-like Labor-Farmer Porty because of dis-
satisfaction with the conservative leadership of the
Socialists. Like Lr. .To Kanju, ir. Juzuki is not a
Marxist, and he has been as opposed to suggested mergers
with the Japan Communist Party as ony of his party's
right wingers. While noted for his inelleotual integrity
and with some reputation as an economie expert, Lir. Suzuki
has given little evidence of being a brilliant or particu-
larly capable political leader. His thcories of socialism
lack clarity As a politiclun, he betrays considerable
confusion in nis thinking. He is unti-capitalistic
without sharing the communists' anti-cupitalism. In
the confused middle ground between right and left, between
capitalism and comaunism, Lr. Suzuki might well lose his
way. Despite his enti-comaunist views, he could be easily
susceptible to a comunist-inspired popular front movement
if it were offered in the right arb.
In moving further to the left, the Social Denocratic
Party has undoubtedly strengthened itself. It will now
be able to bid more successfully for mass labor support.
In this sense, the party may be able to counter more
effectively

The national convention of the Socialists has by
no means erased the heterogeneous, factional character
of the Social Democratic Party. ac! step in the convention's
deliberations, from the election of officers to the
definitions of party policy, was marked by compromises
and adjustments between the left and right wings. But
in this process of connro ise ard adjustment the center
of gravity has definitely shifted to the left, and, while
an open split between the two extremes has been avoided,
the party is still a coalition of conflicting fections.
The inereased strength of the party's left wing
can be attributed only in part to labor's larger role
in party affairs as e result of the national convention.
Equally important is the decline in strength and prestige
of the right wing leaders. From the time the party was

Mr. Yoshida's position is actually a relative one:
Since the end of the war the scale for measuring politioal
viewpoints has been shifted far to the left. During
the war this scale heen on the far right with the militarism
and extreme nationalis  as personified by the Tojo coterie,
and extended only about as far to the left as Iir. Yoshida
and other peace advocates who were harassed or imprisoned
for their "radical" views.. With the end of the war,
almost everything from Tojo extrenism to the edge of
Mr. Yoshida's conservative position was swept from Japanese
political life, while, at the same time, the political
scale was shifted to include newly-risen political groups
ranging from the liberalism of the Social De ocratic
Party to communism. This shifting of the scale has, of
course, left Mir. Yoshida at what has now becone the right
in Japanese politics.
In his appeal for sympathy and understanding, Mr. Yoshida
expressed the hope that the world will reelize that the
Japanese are today sincerely endeavoring to rebuild their
nation upon a peaceful, demooratic he is. Me plealed for
a tempering of thoughts of revenge anl petpib tion by
those who suffered from Japanese aggre  vith the
realization that Japan now stends before he worla chastened,
humbled, and helpless es a result of  siu strous military
defeat. The Prime Minister expressed the hope thet the
world will not hold the transgressions of the past against
Japan's future and will perait Jarun to resuise its place
among the nations. "All we are saking now," the Trime
Minister pleaded, "is access to meteriale ns arkets
so that we may buy the food we need to live."
The Prime Minister's sppeal for sympath: nd under-
standing of the problems which face Japan's recovery
and his denial of heving reactionary policies have been
favorably received by the Jepanese press as a highly
desirable effort to dispel suspicion and misunderstanding
over Japan's efforts to recover. Sofe Japanese, however,
have expressed disappointment thst I. Yoshida did not
deal with the communist problenm and reiterate his
determination to stamp out comiunism. In makins his
appeal for the world's sympathy snd in attempting to counter
charges of being reactionary, Mr. Yoshida probably believed
it would be ineppropriate to deal with the communists
beyond his concluding statement that he end his party do
not see eye to eye with the Socialists and "abhor communism".

sUBCT: Tokyo's 265 of April 28, 1949
Based on the results of the January general
elections, the Central Executive of the JCP has pub-
licly announced the party's new struggle principles.
These principles emphasize popularfranu strategy,
being critical of the party's failure so far to
achieve progress in that direction. They also attack
the Government's plan to introduce foreign ("comprador")
capital into Japan and attack all phases of the stabil.
ization program, particularly its taxation and labor
rationalization phases.
The JCP announcement includes the statement:
"each struggle will emphasize that the choice is
between a democratic people's government, in pther
words seizure of powers by the people, and anhihila-
tion". According to Mr. Spinks Smih, this statement
signifies that previous JCp concepts of "peaceful revo-
lution" have been discarded in favor of a situation of
undisguised revolution by force, but that this strategy
will probably be held in check during the occupation.
Mr. Spinks regards this change of strategy as based on
894.00/5-1849
the election results which encouraged the Communists to
become more aggressive and violent.
The JC? announcement recognizes that an effective
united front will require a skillful harmonizing under
Communist leadershin of varied elements of sunporters
in different occunations. This will require the care-
ful training and ejucation of party members, with emphasis
on "working principles" as opoosed to theory.

Subjedt: OYAMA Iuo
With the exoeption of the lete
NOSAKA Sanzo, Professor OYANA Ikdo
Japan's outstanding political exilo
sojourn in the United States from lo3e to
the middle of the war, he lived quletly at
University, working in.the Politioal Soionoo
under the tutelage and patronage of Professor
COLEGROVE. About 1943-1944, however, as mor
tion began to be given to the shape Japan we
after the war, attention began to center on Pro
Oyama both as a source for information and as a
postwar leader in Japan. American communist a
left-wingers among Americans and Japanesd roail r
United States became convinced thet some kind oaer
popular front would emerge in postwar Japan and begar
approach Professor Oyama as a possible leader.
There is no doubt that Professor. Oyama by this
period was himself swinging much further to the left
than he had ever, been when he engaged in politieal
activities in Japan before l932, and was highly susoeptible
to the advances made by communists in the United States.
Shortly after Japan's surrender and the emergence
of leftist and communist parties in Japan, considerable
agitation began among left-wing Japanese for the purpose
of promoting Professor Oyema's return. This agitation
embraced a rether wide range of political sentiment from
communists end communist sympathizers to such relatively
conserv tive elements as leaders of the Social Demooratio
Party, including Mr. KATaYAMA Tetsu, leter premier, who
signed a petition urging his return.
It is obvious that left-wing politicians in Japan
hoped Professor Oyame would return primarily because they
desired to use him, believing that his reputation would
greatly enhance their parties. The Japan Communist Party
however, at no time expressed openly an opinion on
Oyama's return. Some communists in a personal capacity
welcomed the idea, but available informetion indicates
that the party itself was, at that time, somewhat soeptioal
of Oyama and considered him a political opportunist.
There was also some fear expressed in conmunist circles
that Professor Oyama might be able to organize a new
aggressive left-wing party which would compete with the
Japan Communist Party, or that he might head a popular
front movement which, because of Oyama's reputation and
great appeal, might easily get out of the communists
control. Since his return to Japan, however, the
Japanese

While not a communist sympathizer, Professor Colegrove

The correspondence of Professor Colegrove in

In the meantime, Mr. IUKoYAMA Teruo, a Chicago businessman
who financed Professor Oyama's activities, had arranged
a speaking tour for Oyana on the Pacific West Coast and
in Hawaii. During this tour in July and ugust, 1947
Oyama revealed his sympethies for the com unists and was
In close touch with Japanese and Amerigan communists on
the West Coast and in Hawaii. Professor Colegrove was
alarmed and apparently disillusioned by these developments,
and in his correspondence referred to above expressed
regret that he had been in part instrumental in having
General Whitney "arrange" for Oyama's return to Japan.
It is not known just what these alleged "arrangements"
were. General Whitney has vehemently denied to the
Chief of the Military Intelligence Section that he was
in any way responsible for Oyama's return. So far as
the undersigned can rec ll, at no time during extensive
investigations of Oyama's return to Japan end his subsequent
aotivites was any information developed as to the manner
in whioh arrengements were made for his return, except
that financial arrangements were apparently made by
Mr. Mukoyema who in subsequent intercepted correspondence
has indicated some misgivings on this point.
It is believed that the full story of Professor
Qyama's return to Japan must be found in the files of the
epartment of the Army.

Communist Party Organizational Terminology
Most of the terms listed represent standard
communist nomenclature; aome of the terms, however
are peculiar only to the Japan Communist Party.
Attention is especially invited to section seven
enclosed article which discusses the importance or
standardized terminology in dealing with communist
party organization and activities. This problem has
been considerably complicated in Japan by the peouliari
ties of the Japanese language and the general lack of
understanding among Japanese of the organizational
structure of the comunist party.

Hahelons of the Japen Communist Party
4. Central Headquarters Departments and Bureaus
5. Publications and Documents of Japan Communist Party
6. Congresses, Conferences and Meetings
7. Impcrtance of a Standard JCP Terminology
1. Introdretion:
Th.e crganisational complexity of the Japan Communist Party (Jop)
to this Larg .mber of new tenns employed by the Communist Party is the
necessity fo: trunslating these terms into English. This article is de
signed to establish e standard terminology with the Romaji (Japanese writ
ten in Roman letters) translations which vill end the present confusion
conceming terns used by the JCF and to standardize their usage.
To faci'itate the use of this article as a reference work, the defini
tions of terms have been placed under major classifications. The standord
Romaji names and translations of the :ost important Party terms are listed
with brief explanations. A basic chart, "Comnand Echelons of the Japan
Communist Party" is presented (page SPA - ) to serve as an aid in finding
the Romaji and English translations of JCP organizational terminology. The
standard JCP terms in this article may have other good translations but for
the sake of uniformity, these standard terms are recommended.
The most frequently used mis-translations of JCP terms are described
in order to emphasize the undesirability of the use of such terms. The
standard terminology established in an appendix to the CIS Periodical Sum-
mary No. 34, 15 Nov 48, entitled "Alphabetical Index of Leftist Organiza-
tions in Japan" is not repeated in this article and should be consuited for
that phase of Communist activity.
2. Echelons of the Japan Communist Party:
a. Central Committee (Chuo Tinkai): The Central Comnittee of the JCP
is the national organ charged with the implementation of policies decided
by the National Congress or National Convention. This committee also formu-
lates and executes all necessary policy between the sessions of the National
Congress. All members of the Central Committee are publicly announced and
it is incorrect to refer to all representatives from the JCP Central Head-
quarters as Central Committee members.
b. Regional Committee (Chiho Iinkai): A Re ional Comnittee of the JCP
embraces several Prefect tral Committees and can be consiuered to be opera-
tional in scope raher than administrative. Regional Committees were first
officially autacrized by the Sixth National Congreas of the JCP in Japan.
The Party Constitution provides that the head of a Regional Conmittee will
be elected by the Regional Comnittee but at present each of the chiefs of
the Regional Commiutees is a Central Committee Organizer who is a member of
either the Centrel Committee or of the Central Conmittee Candidate Pool.
Each Regional Committee has a Standing Committee (Jonin Tinkai) which exer-
cises the power of the Regional Comnittee when this committee is not meeting.
Some of the most comnon misnomers for the Regional Connittee are: District
Committee, Great District Conanittee or Large District Committee.
*Note:
The bisic reference for this article is "Guide to Communist
Terminu logy," 11 Feb 49. prepared by the Fourth CIC District.
Referer.ces also nave been made to CIS files for the purpose
of checking and making amplifications.

SPECIAL ARTTOLES
In Hokkaido, the JCF has a Greater Regional Committee (
which corresponds to the standard Regional Committee. Under t
Greater Regional Committee are Regional Comisittees which are
standard Prefectural Committees in the rest of Japen.
c. Prefectural Committee (Ken Tinkai): The Prefectural Committ
the JCP are directly under the Regional Committees and are headed by a Chal
man. Erroneous designations of the Prefectural Committee are: District Bom
mittee, Regional Committee, Branch of the JCP, Prefectural Central.Committee
or Prefectural Council. The Chairman of a Prefectural Committee should not
be referred to as Chief Communist or leading Communist.

d. District Committee (Chiku Iinkai): The immediate supervisory organ
over Cells or Squads is known as the District Committee. This Committee is
responsible for the direction of local propaganda activities and for insur-
ing the participation of all subordinate organs in the various Party campaigns.
The person in charge of the District Committee is called a Chair an. Frequent
errors used in reporting. on this Committee include: Regional Committee,
Branch of a Prefectural Cosmittee or Area Committee.

f. Cell (Saibo) or Squad (Han): The lowest official
Communist Party is the Cell or Squad. At the Sixth National Co
purpose of creating a better public impression by avoiding the ume oft
intemationally used communistic term of Cell. Recently, the Party heo
verted to the use of Cell and both terms are in common usage. A Cell 1o
formed with a minimum of three members who are living or working closm
enough for mutual conmin cation. No maximum size has been esteblished for
Cells and some Cells report as many as 80 members. A coanon crroneous
designetion for Ce! js is .Lo al Pranch.
g. Fraction ( Furaxu) (Tritten in Katakana): The mos confusing of
Communist terms ar- the woras Fiaction and Faction. A Fraction is an im-
portant Party term enc means a group of JCP members vho may also be organ-
ized into one or more cells ho are placed into or recruitea from'a target
organization over which the Party wishes to establish contiol. Thus, a
Fraction is the unit of infiltration of the Party. In Jepan, a Fraction
may be either revealed or it ma operate uncer cover. In Japanese labor
unions, there are instances of botn a concealed and open Fraction in the
same union. An open Fraction generally operates under the name of Manage
ment Cell: and issues orders end publications in the name of the Cell. A
Fraction is not an actual. echelon in the Party organization but can be. at-
tached to the formal str cture at any level. In certain large target
groups such as the Japan Farmers Union, the Fractions are pyramided into
echelons with the various levels of the echelon maintainine liaison and
contact with the corresponding echelon of the Party. The revised term for
Fraction is Group (Gurupu) ("ritten in Katakana) but Fraction still re-.
mains in general use.
The word Faction has no particular meaning in Communist terminology
and it has the normal meeni of two or more groups with opposing ideas in
the same organization. This term is used in many types of orgenizations,
both Communist and non-Com unist. Followers of the severel cliques in the
JCP or partisans of certain leaders are properly known as Factions, such
as the Yenan Faction, the Nosaka Faction or the Prison Faction.
3. Communist Position Titles:
a. Secretary General (Shoki Cho): The standard int rnational Commu-
nist term for the execitive heao of the Party within each country is Secre-
tary General. Chief Clerk or Head Clerk are not correct interpretations of
this position title.
b. Members of Corrnit  es: The best usage in referring to the posi-
tions of members of any o inist Committee is to say member of the Central
Committee, Kanto Regiona. Jcrnitteeman or member of th Control Committee.
The use of such terms as Prei ctural Executive or Dist ct Director for mem-

4. Central Headquarters Departments and Bureaus:
The Departments and Bureaus of the JCP Central Headquarters are partly
duplicated in the lower echelon headquarters of the Party. There is no
fixed organization of these Departments and Bureaus in the various Regional
Prefectural or District headquarters and they are established when the local
situation makes them necessary. The same names and translations are general-
ly useá.
a. Secretariat (Shoki Kyoku): The central administrative agency for
the JCP is the Secretariat. The main duties of the Secretariat are to issue
official Directives and Notifications in the name of the Central Committee;
to keep official statistics on the internal affairs of the Party; and to pro-
vide an accounting of finances on a national basis. most freguently the
Secretariat is misnamed the Clerk Bureau, Clerical Bureau, Clerk Section,
Secretary Department of Secretary's Pool.
b. Political Bureau or Politburo (Seiji Kyoku): The Political Bureau
is the Party agency authorized to decide general and political policies for
the Party's activities. These decisions are subject to a theoretical re-
view and approval by the Central Comittee when in session. The Political
Bureau has been misnamed the Political Board, the Political Affairs Section
and the Political Department.
c. Organization and Activities Bureau (Soshiki Katsudo Shido Bu): The
Organization and Activities Bureau of Central Headouarters makes general
daily decisions on tactical measures and also aids in the formulation of
propa anda keynotes and tactical guides. Although other translations of
this term may be correct, the title of Organization and Activities Bureau
best describes this agency. Incorrect translations are Organization Action
and Leadership Department and the Formation and Activity Guidance Section.
d. Control Committee (Tosei Iinkai): The Control Committee of the JCP
Central Headquarters is the watchdog of the headquarters. Tts duties in-
clude inspection and supervision of the implementation of decisions of the
National Congress and the Central Committee, enforcement of Party regula-
tions and defense against anti-Communist elements who seek to destroy the
Party. The Committee holds regular general meetings at least once a month
and conducts joint meetings with the Political Bureau when important urgent
matters arise. It is composed of not more than 10 members who are elected
by the National Congress.
e. Central Committee Candidate Pool (Chuo Jin Kono): Members of the
Central Committee Candidate Pool are elected by the JCP National Congress.
Provisions are made for a total of 25 persons to be held in this pool and
whenever a vacancy occurs in the Central Committee, a Central Committee
Candidate is selected for that position.

5. Publications and Documents of the JCP:
a. Directives (Shirei) and Notifications (Tsutatsu): The Secretariat
normally issues all Party orders in the form of Directives or Notifications
although the Political Bureau and the Control Committee occasionally pub-
lish Directives and Notifications. The majority of these orders are num-
bered serially to facilitate filing but at times are published without num-
bers. These terms of Directives and Notifications are to be used instead
of Instruction or Notice.
b. Organization and Propaganda Guide (Soshiki Senden Shishin): The
Organization and Propaganda Guide is a joint publication of the Organization
and Activities Bureau and the Propaganda and Education Departments. It ap-
pears irregularly every 10 or 15 days and is considered to be a registered
and classified document by the JCF.
c. Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin (Soshiki Katsudo
Sokuho): This bulletin is published by the Organization and Activities
Bureau at weekly intervals at present. It is a relatively new publication
which contains news and irs tructions concerning the pending Communist
"struggles." The Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin is reported-
ly another JCP registered document.
d. Guide to Party Activity (To Katsudo Shishin): The Propaganda and
Education Department of the JCP headquarters publishes the Guide to Party
Activity. This guide is a new Party publication anc is a registered JCP
document. It is reportedly published to supplement the Organization and
Propaganda Guide.

names.

7.
Impcrtance of a Standard JCP Terminology
Introctction:
The crganisational complexity of the Japan Communist Party (JCP) has
produced & w.ce variation of terminology peculiar only to the JCP. Added
to this largo imber of new tenns emploved by the Communist Party is the
necessity for tran-lating these terms into English. This article is de-
signed to estabiish a standard terminology with the Romaji (Japanese writ-
ten in Roman latters) translations which vill end the present confusion
concerning terms nsed by the JCF and to standardize their usage.
To facilitate the use of this article as a reference work, the defini-
tions of terms have been placea under major classifications. The standard
Romaji names and translations of the most important Party terms are listed
with brief explanations. A basic chart, "Comnand Echelons of the Japan
Communist Party" is presented (page SPA - ) to serve as an aid in finding
the Romaji and English translations of JCP orgenizational terminology. The
standard JCP terms in this article may have other good translations but for
the sake of uniformity, these standard terms are recommended.
The most frequently used mis-translations of JCP terms are described
in order to emphasize the undesirability of the use of such terms. The
standard terminology established in an appendix to the CIS Periodical Sun-
mary No. 34, 15 Nov 48, entitled "Alphabetical Index of Leftist Organiza-
tions in Japan" is not repeated in this article and should be consuited for
that phase of Communist activity.
2. Echelons of the Japan Communist Party:
a. Central Committee (Chuo Iinkai): The Central Committce of the JCP
is the national organ charged with the implementation of policies decided
by the National Congress or National Convention. This committee also formu-
lates and executes all necessary policy between the sessions of the National
Congress. All members of the Central Committee are publicly announced and
it is incorrect to refer to all representatives from the JCP Central Head-
quarters as Central Committee members.
b. Regional Committee (Chiho Iinkai): A Regional Committee of the JCP
embraces several F efect ural Comrittees and can be considered to be opera-
tional in scope  her than administrative. Regional Committees were first
officially autacrized by the Sixth National Congress of the JCP in Japan.
The Party Constitution provides that the heaa of a Regional Committee will
be elected by the Regional Committee but at present each of the chiefs of
the Regional Commiutees is a Central Committee Organizer who is a member of
either the Central Committee or of the Central Committee Candidate Pool.
Each Regional Committee has a Standing Comrittee (Jonin Tinkai) which exer-
cises the power of the Regional Comnittee when this committee is not meeting.
Some of the most comnon misnomers for the Regional Comaittee are: District
Committee, Great District Comnittee or Large District Committee.
* Note:
The besic reference for this article is "Guide to Communist
Terminclogy," 1l Feb 49, prepared by the Fourth CIC District.
Refererces also have been made to CIS files for the purpose
of checking and making amplifications.

which corresponds to the stardard Regional Committee. Under th
Greater Regional Committee are Regional Committees which are pe
standard Prefectural Committees in the rest of Japan.
c. Prefectural Committee (Ken Tinkai): The Prefectural Committaba
the JCP are directly under the Regional Committees and are headed by a Chair
man. Erroneous designations of the Prefectural Committee are: District Com
mittee, Regional Committee, Branch of the JCP, Prefectural Central Committee
or Prefectural Council. The Chairman of a Prefectural Committee should not
be referred to as Chief Communist or leading Communist.

d. District Committee (Chiku Iinkai): The immediate supervisory organ
over Cells or Squads is known as the District Committee. This Committee is
responsible for the direction of local propaganda activities and for insur-
ing the participation of all subordinate organs in the various Party campaigns.
The person in charge of the District Committee is called a Chair an. Frequent
errors used in reporting on this Committee include: Regional Committee,
Branch of a Prefectural Comittee or Area Committee.

e. Cell Group (Saibo Gun): The Cell Group or Squad Group
activities is desirable. A Cell Group is authorized to have a Cell try
or Squad Group Committee. Cell Groups are often misnamed Joint Cells oh
Cell Group. Squads.
f. Cell (Saibo) or'Squad (Han): The lowest official echelon of: the
Communist Party is the Cell or Squad. At the Sixth National Convention of
the JCP it was decided that the name Cell would be changed to Squad for the
purpose of creating a better public impression by avoiding the use of the
internationally used communistic term of Cell. Recently, the Party has re-
verted to the use of Cell and both terms are in common usage. A Cell is
formed with a minimum of three members who are living or working close
enough for mutual comnin cation. No maximum size has been esteblishtd for
Cells and some C.i!s report as many as 80 members. A coanon crroneous
designation for Ce:]s js Lo al Franch.
g Fraction (Furaku) (Vritten in Katakana): The mos't confusing of
Communist terms are tne words Fraction and Faction. A Fraction is an im-
portant Party tern enc means a ¿roup of JCP members who may also be organ-
ized into one or more cells  ho are placed into or recruitea from a target
organization over vhich the Farty vishes to establish control. Thus, a
Fraction is the unit of infiltration of the Party. In Japan, = Fraction
may be either revealed or it may operate uncer cover. In Japanese labor
unions, there are instances of both a' concealed and open Fraction in the
same union. An open Fraction generally operates under the name of Manage-
ment Cell and issues orders and publications in the name of the Cell. A
Fraction is not an actual echelon in the Party organization but can be at-
tached to the formal structure at any level. In certain large target
groups such as the Japan Farmers Union, the Fractions are pyramided into
echelons with the various lovels of the echelon maintaining liaison and
contact with the corresponding echelon of the Party. The revised term for
Fraction is Group (Gurupu) ("ritten in Katakana) but Fraction still re-
mains in general use.
The word Faction has no particular meaning in Communist terminology
and it has the normal meaning of two or more groups with opposing ideas in
the same organization. This term is used in many types of organizations,
both Communist and non-Com unist. Followers of the several cligues in the
JCP or partisans of certain leaders are properly known as Factions, such
as the Yenan Faction, the Nosaka Faction or the Prison Factior

are designated as Organizers. Although every Party member has tho duty of

considered as Organizers and every Communist agitator should not be referred

6. Congresses, Conferences and Meetings:
a.. National Level:
(1) National Congress or National Convention (Zenkoku Taikal)
regular JCP National Congress or National Convention must be held at Least
once each year according to Party regulations. Emergency sessions may be
called when more than one-third of the members of the Central Committee or
one-half of the delegates to the previous National Congress so demand.. The
National Congress is the highest policy-making body of the JCP anc is empow-

of the Congress. It also selects members of the Central Committee, the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool and the Control Comittee. The National Con-

7. Importance of a Standard JCp Terminology:
The adoption of a standard organizational terminology for the Japan Com-
munist Party has many advantages and a definite importance. First, in order
to increase the comprehensibility of intelligence reports, a fixed and uniform
method of reporting Communist terms becomes necessary. At times a well prepared
report is rendered less valuable by confusing and erroneous Party terms which
are only understood by the person preparing the report. An added difficulty
is the fact that the Japanese language has produced a wide variation of names
for Communist terns so that thore ma, be several pessible translations for one
such term.
In this report an attempt also has been made to establish a standard term-
inology which ill conforr as much as possible to the conmon international us-
age of Conmunist terms which are employed by Communist Parties in countries
all over the world. The use of this article as a reference work for official
standard organizational terminology of the JCP should contribute to a. greater
clarification and ease of comprehersion of all articles and reports pertaining
to the JCP.

7. Impcrtance of a Standard JCP Terminology
1. Introcration:
The crganisational complexity of the Japan Communist Party (JCP) has
produced & w.ce variation of terminology peculiar only to the JCP. Added
to this largo limber of new tenns employed by the Communist Party is the
necessity io, translating these terms into English. This article is de-
signed to establish a standard terminology with the Romaji (Japanese writ-
ten in Roman le'ters) translations which vill end the present confusion
concerming terns used by the JCF and to standardize their usage.
To facilitate the use of this article as a reference work, the defini-
tions of terms have been placed under major classifications. The standard
Romaji names and translations of the most important Party tems are listed
with brief explanations. A basic chart, "Comnand Echelons of the Japan
Communist Party" is presented (pa e SPA - ) to serve as an aid in finding
the Romaji and English translations of JCP organizational terminology. The
standard JCP terms in this article may have other good translations but for
the sake of uniformity, these standard terms are recommended.
The most frequently used mis-translations of JCP terms are described
in order to emphasize the undesirability of the use of such terms. The
standard terminology established in an appendix to the CIS Periodical Sum-
mary No. 34, 15 Nov 48, entitled "Alphabetical Index of Leftist Organiza-
tions in Japan" is not repeated in this article and should be consuited for
that phase of Communist activity.
2. Echelons of the Japan Communist Party:
a. Central Committee (Chuo Tinkai): The Central Comnittee of the JCP
is the national organ charged with the implementation of policies decided
by the National Congress or National Convention. This committee also formu-
lates and executes all necessary policy between the sessions of the National
Congress. All members of the Central Coma ittee are publicly announced and
it is incorrect to refer to all representatives from the JCP Cêntral Head-
quarters as Central Committee members.
b. Regional Committee (Chiho Tinkai): A Regional Committee of the JCP
embraces several F efectural Conurittees and can be considered to be opera-
tional in scop. ra her than administrative. Regional Committees were first
officially autacriced by the Sixth National Congress of the JCP in Japan.
The Party Constitation provides that the heaa of a Regicnal Committee will
be elected by the Regional Committee but at present each of the chiefs of
the Regional Commictees is a Central Committee Organizer who is a member of
either the Central Committee or of the Central Committee Candidate Pool.
Eaeh Regional Committee has a Standing Comrittee (Jonin Tinkai) which exer-
cises the power of the Regional Comnittee when this committee is not meeting.
Some of the most comnon misnomers for the Regional Conuittee are: District
Committee, Great District Coninittee or Large District Committee.
Note:
The bisic reference for this article is "Guide to Communist
Terninclogy," 11 Feb 49, prepared by the Fourth CIC District.
Refererces also have been made to CIS files for the purpose
of checking and making amplifications.

In Hokkaido, the JCP has a Greater Regional Committee (Dai Chiho li
which corresponds to the standard Regional Committee. Under the Hokkal
Greater Regional Committee are Regional Cominittees which are parallel to
standard Prefectural Committees in the rest:of Japan.
c. Prefectural Committee (Ken Iinkai): The Prefectural Committees of
the JCP are di rectly under the Regional Committees and are headed by a. Ehair-
man. Erroneous designations of the Prefectural Committee are: .District Com-
mittee, Regional Committee, Branch of the JCP, Prefectural Central Committee
or Prefectural Council. The Chairman of a Prefectural Committee should not
be referred to as Chief Communist or leading Communist.

d. District Committee (Chiku Iinkai): The immediate supervisory organ
over Cells or Squads is known as the District Committee. This Committee is
responsible for the direction of local propaganda activities and for insur-
ing the participation of all subordinate organs in the various Party campaigns.
The person in charge of the District Committee is called a Chair an. Frequent
errors used in reporting on this Committee include: Regional Conmittee,
Branch of a Prefectural Conmittee or Area Committee.

SPECIALARTICLES
e. Cell Group (Saibo Gun): The Cell Group or Squaa Gmup
activities is desirable. A Cell Group is authorized to have a.Gel
or Squad Group Committee. Cell Groups are often misnamed Joint Callaor
Cell Group Squads.
f. Cell (Saibo) or Squad (Han): The lowest official echelon of the
Communist Party is the Cell or Squad. At the Sixth National Convention of
the JCP it was decided that the neme Cell would be changed to Squad for the
purpose of creating a better public impression by avoiding the use of the
intemationally used communistic term of Cell. Recently, the Party has re-
verted to the use of Cell and both terms are in common usage. A Cell is
formed with a minimum of three members who are living or working close
enough for mutual comarnication. No maximum size has been esteblished for
Cells and some Cc! s eport as many as 80 members. A coanon erroneous
designation for Ce. Js is Lo al Pranch.
g Fraction (Furaki) (Vritten in.Katakana): The mos' confusing of
Communist terms ar? the words Fraction and Faction. A Fraction is an im-
portant Party term ena meaas a group of JCP members vho may also be organ-
ized into one or more cells ho are placed into or recruitea from a target
organization over which the Party wishes to establish control. Thus, a
Fraction is the unit of infiltration of the Party. In Japan, a Fraction
may be either revealed or it mey cperate under cover. In Japanese labor
unions, there are instances of botn a concealed and open Fraction in the
same union. An open Fraction generally operates under the name of Manage-
ment Cell and issues orders and publications in the name of the Cell. A
Fraction is not an actual echelon in the Party organization but can be at-
tached to the formal structure at any level. In certain large target
groups such as the Japan Farmers Union, the Fractions are pyramided into
echelons with the various levels of the echelon maintaining liaison and
contact with the corresponding echelon of the Party. The revised term for
Fraction is Group (Gurupu) (Written in Katakana) but Fraction still re-
mains in general use.
The word Faction has no particular meaning in Conmunist terminology
and it has the normal meening of two or more groups with opposing ideas in
the same organization. This term is used in many types of organizations,
both Communist and non-Communist. Followers of the several cliques in the
JCP or partisans of certain leaders are properly known as Factions, such
as the Yenan Faction, the Nosaka Faction or the Prison Faction.
3. Communist Position Titlcs:
a. Secretary General (Shcki Cho): The standard international Commu-
nist term for the executive head of the Party within each country is Secre-
tary General. Chief Clerk or Head Clerk are not correct interpretations of
this position title.
b. Members of Commitcees: The best usage in referring to the posi-
tions of members of any (o munist Committee is to say member of the Central
Committee, Kanto Regiona. Jonnitteeman or member of the Control Committee.
The use of such terms as Prefectural Executive or Dist  ct Director for mem-
bers of committees is erroncous and confusing.
c. Organizers or Ors (Orugu) (Written in Katakana): The term Organi-
zer refers to a specific position in the Communist Party and certain persons
are designated as Organizers. Although cvery Party member has tho duty of
recruiting new members and organizing now groups, thcy are not officially
considered as Organizers and every Communist agitator should not be referred
to as an Organizer. Organizers are normally officially assigned to echelons

Central Headquarters Departments and Bureaus:
The Departments and Bureaus of the JCP Central Headquartera.arerpartly
duplicated in the lower echelon headquarters of the Party. There is no
fixed organization of these Departments and Bureaus in the various Regional,
Prefectural or District headquarters and they are established when the local
situation makes them necessary. The same names and translations are general-
ly used.
a. Secretariat (Shoki Kyoku): The central administrative agency for
the JCP is the Secretariat. The main duties of the Secretariat are to issue
official Directives and Notifications in the name of the Central Committee;
to keep official statistics on the internal affairs of the Party; and to pro-
vide an accounting of finances on a national basis. Most freguently the
Secretariat is misnamed the Clerk Bureau, Clerical Bureau, Clerk Section,
Secretary Department of Secretary's Pool.
b. Political Bureau or Politburo (Seiji Kyoku): The Political Bureau
is the Party agenc: authorized to decide general and political policies for
the Party's activities. These decisions are subject to a theoretical re-
view and approval by the Central Comnittee when in session. The Political
Bureau has been misnamed the Political Board, the Political Affairs Section
and the Political Department.
c. Organization and Activities Bureau (Soshiki Katsudo Shido Bu): The
Organization and Activities Bureau of Central Headguarters makes general
daily decisions on tactical measures and also aids in the formulation of
propa anda keynotes and tactical guides. Although other translations of
this term may be correct, the title of Organization and Activities Bureau
best describes this agency. Incorrect translations are Organization Action
and Leadership Department and the Formation and Activity Guidance Section.
d. Control Committee (Tosei Iinkai): The Control Committee of the JCP
Central Headquarters is the watchdog of the headquarters. Tts duties in-
clude inspection and supervision of the implementation of decisions of the
National Congress and the Central Committee, enforcement of Party regula-
tions and defense against anti-Communist elements who seek to destroy the
Party. The Committee holds regular general meetings at least once a month
and conducts joint meetings with the Political Bureau when important urgent
matters arise. It is composed of not more than 10 members who are elected
by the National Congress.
e. Central Committee Candidate Pool (Chuo Tin Koho): Members of the
Central Committee Candidate Pool ere elected by the JCP National Congress.
Provisions are made for a total of 25 persons to be held in this pool and
whenever a vacancy occurs in the Central Committee, a Central Committee
Candidate is selected for that position.

5. Publications and Documents of the JCP:
a. Directives (Shirei) and Notifications (Tsutatsu): The Secretariat
normally issues all Party orders in the form of Directives or Notifications
although the Political Bureau and the Control Committee occasionally pub-
lish Directives and Notifications. The majority of these orders are num-
bered serially to facilitate filing but at times are published without num-
bers. These terms of Directives and Notifications are to be used instead
of Instruction or Notice.
b. Organization and Propaganda Guide (Soshiki Senden Shishin): The
Organization and Propaganda Guide is a joint publication of the Organization
and Activities Bureau and the Propaganda and Education Departments. It ap-
pears irregularly every 10 or 15 days and is considered to be a registered
and classified document by the JCF.
c. Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin (Soshiki Katsudo
Sokuho): This bulletin is publishea by the Organization and Activities
Bureau at weekly intervals at present. It is a relatively new publication
which contains news and is tructions concerning the pending Communist
"struggles." The Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin is reported-
ly another JCP registered document.
d. Guide to Party Activity (To Katsudo Shishin): The Propaganda and
Education Department of the JCP headquarters publisnes the Guide to Party
Activity. This guide is a new Party publication and is a registered JCP
document. It is reportedly published to supplement the Organization and
Propaganda Guide.
e. Official Party Publications: (For detailed information on JCP
publications see CIS Periodical Summary No. 31, 15 Aug 48, Page SUM - 23)
(1) Akahata (Red Flag): The official daily newspaper organ of
the JCP is Akahata.
This newspaper is published for both public and Party
member consumption.
The term Akahata is generaily used without the trans-
lation.
(2) Zenei (Vanguard): Zenei is the official monthly publicetion
of the Conmunist Party and is for public consumptio.. Similarly to Akahata,
the term Zenei is usually used witho't the translation.
(3) Other Farty Organs and Publications: All echelons of the JCP
publish organ papers for education of members and public propaganda. Trans-
lation of these titles will normally produce standard and easily understood
names.
Central Headquarters Departments of the JCP also publish a large number
of pamphlets and Jeaflets which are prepared for public newsstand distribu-
tion for the purpose of propaganda and fund raising campaigns. Most of
these publications are issued by the Publications Department of the JCP but
other Central Headquarters Departments also issue such publications.

Congresses, Conferences and Meetings:
a.
National Level:
(1) National Congress or National Convention (Zenkokuralka)
regular JCP National Congress or National Convention must be hold t
once each year according to Party regulations. Emergency sessions may be
called when more than one-third of the members of the Central Committeo or
one-half of the delegates to the previous National Congress so demand. The
National Congress is the highest policy-making body of the JCP and is empow-

of the Congress. It also selects members of the Central Committee, the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool and the Control Committee. The National Con-
gress is attended by members of the Central Committee, the Central Committee
Candidate Pool and the Cortrol Committee together with representatives from
the Prefectural Committees. National Congress is preferred, rather than Na-
tional Convention.

the JCP headquarters has the authority to call & National Conference to dis-

the National Congress. A National Conference is composed of the Central Com-

tional Conference as the authority to remove up to one-fifth of the total
membershi. of the Central Committee for. cause, to elevate members of tne Cen-

Committee created by expulsion of undesirable members anc then to select addi-
tional members for the Candidate Pool to fill the vacancies in that group.
(3) General Meeting (Sokai): The regulations of the JCP provide
that a General Meeting will be held at least once every four months. This
General Meeting is composed of the Central Committee plus members of the Cen-
tral Conmittee Candidate Pool. The mectins has the power to select members
for the Political Bureau and for the Secretariat fron among the Central Com-
mittee membership and also conducts discussions on national strategy and
tactics oí the Party.
(4) Central Commi tee Meeting (Chuo Iinkai): The Central Committee
is required to hold ree lar mestings at least once a month accordins to JCP
regulations. At these mectin's decisions are made relative to routine Party
affairs but due to the presonce of many influential members on the conmittee,
policy decisions of great importance may be decided at regular Central Commit-
tee Meetings..

quarters as Central Committee members.
b. Regional Committee (Chiho Iinkai): A Regional Committee of the JCP
embraces several Frefectural Committees and can be considered to be opera-
tional in seope ra her than administrative. Regional Committees were first
officially autacrized by the Sixth National Congress of the JCP in Japan.
The Party Constitulion provides that the head of a Regicnal Committee will
be elected by the Regional Committee but at present each of the chiefs of
the Regional Commiutees is a Central Committee Organizer who is a member of
either the Centrel Committee or of the Central Committee Candidate Pool.
Each Regional Committee has a Standing Committee (Jcnin Tinkai) which exer-
cises the power of the Regional Committee when this committee is not meeting.
Some of the most common misnomers for the Regional Committee are: District
Committee, Great District Comnittee or Large District Committee.

f. Cell (Saibo) or Squad (Han): The lowest official echelon of the
Communist Party is the Cell or Squad. At the Sixth National Convention of
the JCP it was decided that the name Cell would be changed to Squad for the
purpose of creating a better public impression by avoiding the use of the
internationally used communistic term of Cell. Recently, the Party has re-
verted to the use of Cell and both terms are in common usage. A Cell is
formed with a minimum of three members who are living or working close
enough for mutual cemminication. No maximum size has been esteblished for
Cells and some Cclis eport as many as 80 members.. A coianon erroneous
designation for Ce!js. js Lo al Pranch.
g. Fraction (Furaku) (Tritten in Katakana): The most confusing of
Communist terms ar- the words Fraction and Faction. A. Fraction is an im-
portant Party tera enc means a group of JCP members vho may also be organ-
ized into one or more cells ho are placed into or recruitea from a target
organization over which the Party wishes to establish control. Thus, a
Fraction is the unit of infiltration of the Party. In Japan, a Fraction
may be either revealed or it may operate under cover. In Japanese labor
unions, there are instances of both a concealed and open Fraction in the
same union. An open Fraction generally operates under the name of Manage-
ment Cell and issues orders and publications in the name of the Cell. A
Fraction is not an actual echelon in the Party organization-but can be at-
tached to the formal structure at any level. In certain large target
groups such as the Japan Farmers Union, the Fractions are pyramided into
echelons with the various levels of the echelon maintaining liaison and
contact with the corresponding eckelon of the Party. The revised term for
Fraction is Group (Gurupu) (ritten in Katakana) but Fraction still re-
mains in general use.
The word Faction has no particular meaning in Communist terminology
and it has the normal meaning of two. or more groups with opposing ideas in
the same organization. This term is used in many types of organizations,
both Communist and non-Comunist. Followers of the several cliques in the
JCP or partisans of certain leaders are properly known as Factions, such
as the Yenan Faction, the Nosaka Faction or the Prison Faction.

4.
Central Headquarters Departments and Bureaus:
The Departments and Bureaus of the JCP Central Headquarters are partly
duplicated in the lower echelon headquarters of the Party. There is no
fixed organization of these Departments and Bureaus in the various Regional,
Prefectural or District headquarters and they are established when the local
situation makes them necessary. The same names and translations are general-
ly usea.
a. Secretariat (Shoki Kyoku): The central administrative agency for
the JCP is the Secretariat. The main duties of the Secretariat are to issue
official Directives and Notifications in the name of the Central Committee;
to keep official statistics on the internal affairs of the Party; and to pro-
vide an accounting of finances on a national basis. most freguently the
Secretariat is misnamed the Clerk Bureau, Clerical Bureau, Clerk Section,
Secretary Department of Secretary's Pool.
b. Political Bureau or Politburo (Seiji Kyoku): The Political Bureau
is the Party agency authorized to decide general and political policies for
the Party's activities. These decisions are subject to a theoretical re-
view and approval by the Centrel Comittee when in session. The Political
Bureau has been misnamed the Political Board, the Political Affairs Section
and the Political Department.
c. Organization and Activities Bureau (Soshiki Katsudo Shido Bu): The
Organization and Activities Bureau of Central Headguarters makes general
daily decisions on tactical measures and also aids in the formulation of
propa anda keynotes and tactical guides. Although other translations of
this term may be correct, the title of Organization and Activities Bureau
best describes this agency. Incorrect translations are Organization Action
and Leadership Department and the Formation and Activity Guidance Section.
d. Control Committee (Tosei Iinkai): The Control Committee of the JCP
Central Headquarters is the watchdog of the headquarters. Tts duties in-
clude inspection and supervision of the implementation of decisions of the
National Congress and the Central Committee, enforcement of Party regula-
tions and defense against anti-Communist elements who seek to destroy the
Party. The Committee holds regular general meetings at least once a month
and conducts joint meetings with the Political Bureau when important urgent
matters arise. It is composed of not more than 10 members who are elected
by the National Congress.
e. Central Comnittee Candidate Pool (Chuo Iin Koho): Members of the
Central Committee Candidate Pool are elected by the JCP National Congress.
Provisions are made for a total of 25 persons to be held in this pool and
whenever a vacancy occurs in the Central Comnittee, a Central Committee
Candidate is selected for that position.
