5. Publications and Documents of the JCP:
a. Directives (Shirei) and Notifications (Tsutatsu): The Secretariat
normally issues all Party orders in the form of Directives or Notifications
although the Political Bureau.and the Control Committee occasionally pub-
lish Directives and Notifications. The majority of these orders are num-
bered serially to facilitate filing but at times are published without num-
bers. These terms of Directives and Notifications are to be used instead
of Instruction or Notice.
b. Organization and Propaganda Guide (Soshiki Senden Shishin): The
Organization and Propaganda Guide is a joint publication of the Organization
and Activities Bureau and the Propaganda and Education Departments. It ap-
pears irregularly every 10 or 15 days and is considered to be a registered
and classified document by the JCF.
c. Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin (Soshiki Katsudo
Sokuho): This bulletin is published by the Organization and Activities
Bureau at weekly intervals at present. It is a relatively new publication
which contains news and is tructions concerning the pending Communist
"struggles." The Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin is reported-
ly another JCP registered document.
d. Guide to Party Activity (To Katsudo Shishin): The Propaganda and
Education Department of the JCP headquarters publishes the Guide to Party
Activity. This guide is a new Party publication and is a registered JCP
document. It is reportedly published to supplement the Organization and
Propaganda Guide.
e. Official Party Publications: (For detailed information on JCP
publications see CIS Periodical Summary No. 31, 15 Aug 48, Page SUm - 23).
(1) Akahata (Red Flag): The official daily newspaper organ of
the JCP-is Akahata.
This newspaper is published for both public and Party
member consum,tion.
The term Akahata is generally used without the trans-
lation.
(2) Zenei (Vanguard): Zenei is the official monthly publication
of the Communist Party and is for public consumptio.. Similarly to Akahata,
the term Zenei is usually used witho't the translation.
(3) Other Party Organs and Publications: All echelons of the JCP
publish organ papers for education of members and public propaganda. Trans-
lation of these titles will normally produce standard and easily understood
names.
Central Headquarters Departments of the JCP also publish a large number
of pamphlets and Jeaflets which are prepared for public newsstand distribu-
tion for the purpose of propaganda and fund raising campaigns. Most of
these publications are issued by the Publications Department of the JCP.but
other Central Headquarters Departments also issue such publications.

National Level:
(1) National Congress or National Convention (Zenkok
regular JCP National Congress or National Convention must ba held
once each year according to Party regulations. Emergency sesaions
called when more than one-third of the members of the Central Committ
one-half of the delegates to the previous National Congress so domand The
National Congress is the highest policy-making body of the JCP and 1s empow
ered to decide basic policies which continue in effect until the next meeting
of the Congress. It also selects members of the Central Committee; the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool and the Control Committee. The National Con-
gress is attended by members of the Central Committee, the Central Committee
Candidate Pool and the Cortrol Committee together with representatives from
the Frefectual Committees. National Congress is preferred, rather than Na-
tional Convention.
(2) National Conference (Zenkoku Kaigi): The Central Committee of
the JCP headquarters has the authority to call a National Conference to dis-
cuss urgent Party policies and issues vhich may arise in between sessions of
the National Cong: ss. A National Conference is composed of the Central Com-
mittee plus representatives selected by the Prefectural Committees.. The.Na-
tional Conference as the authority to rerove up to one-fifth of the total
membershi of the Central Committee for cause, to elevate members of the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool to fill the vacancies createa in the Central
Committee created by expulsion of undesirable members and then to select addi-
tional members for the Ca:didate Pool to fill the vacancies in that group.
(3) General Meeting (Sokai): The regulations of the JCF provide
that a General Meeting will be held at least once every four months. This
General Meeting is composed of the Central Committee plus members of the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool. The meetins has the power to select members
for the Political Bureau and for the Secretariat from among the Central Com-
mittee membership and also conducts discussions on national strategy and
tactics oi the Party.
(4) Central Committee Meeting (Chuo Tinkai): The Central Committee
is required to hold regular meetings at least once a month according to JCP
regulations. At these meetines decisions are made relative to routine Party
affairs but due to the presence of many influential members on the committee,
policy decisions of great importance may be decided at regular Central Comait-
tee Meeti.gs.
b. Regional Level:
(1) Regional Party Conference (Chiho To Kaigi): The supreme policy-
making body on the regional level is the Regional Farty Conference. This con-
ference is attended by menbers of the Regienal Conmittee, the Regional Coamit-
tee Candidate Pool, reprasentatives of the Prefectural Committees within the
region and represe. tatives of the Central Committee. The Regional Party Con-
ference establishes plans based on the policies of the Central Committee and
selects members of the Regional Conmittee and the Rugional Committee Candidate
Pool. Held once sach yecr, the Regional Party Co ference ma be called into
emergeney seasion upon directio. of the Regional Com ittee or by request of
more than one-third of the Prefectural Committees.
(2) Regional Committee General Meeting (Chiho Iin Sokai): A Regional
Committee General Meeting is held at least once very three months and is com-
posed of members of the Regional Committee and the Regional Committee Candidate
Pool. The General Mecting has the authority to fill vacancies in the Regional
Committee by selecting replacements from the Regional Committee Candidate Pool.
All such selections are subject to approval by the Central Committee.

(3) Regional Committee Meeting (Chiho Tinkaty

c. Prefectural Level:
(1) Prefectural Party Conference (Ken To Kaigi): The Prefectural
Party Conference corresponds to the National Congress or Regional Party Con
ference and must be convened once a year. Members of the Prefectural Commit
tee, Prefectural Committee Candidate Pool and representatives of subordinate
District Committees make up the Prefectural Party Conference. This conference
elects members of the Prefectural Committee subject to Central Committee ap
proval and establishes plans for the prefecture following policies set by the
Central and Regional Committees. An emergency session of the Prefectural
Party Conference may be called upon decision of the Prefectural Committee or
upon request of more than one-third of the District Committees.
(2) Prefectural Committee General Meeting (Ken Iin Sokai): The Pre-

and is composed of members of the Prefectural Committee and the Prefectural

a month the Prefectural Conmittee meeting is charged with the general supervi-
sion of Party activities in the prefecture. The Prefectural Committee selects
a standing committee to handle daily affairs of the Party and may also estab-
lish special departments to su ervise specific Party functions. The terminol-
ogy of these departments usually is the same as for the Central Headquarters!
Departments.
d. District Level: The highest body of the JCP Districts is the Dis-
trict Party Conference (Chiku To Kaixi) which meets twice each year by order
of the District Committee. Special sessions may be called by either the di-
rection of the District Committee or by reguest of over one-third of the Party
members under the jurisdiction of the District. In between meetings of the
District Party Conference, business is carried on by meetings of the District
Committee. Meetings of Cell Groups and Cells are held as needed but are
usually convened at re ular intervals. Fractions or Group Leetiass normally
termed as Gurupu Kaigi are held when their business dictates. A frequent mis-
translation for this torm is Group Council.
7. Importance of a Standard JCF Terminology:
The adoption of a standard organizational tertinology for the Japan Com-
munist Party has many advantages and a definite importance. First, in order
to increase the comprehensibility of intelligence reports, a fixed and uniform
method of reporting Cormunist terms becomes necessary. At times a well prepared
report is rendered less valuable by confusing and erroneous Party terms which
are only understood by the person preparing the report. An added difficulty
is the fact that the Japanese language has produced a wide variation of names
for Communist terms so that there may be several possible translations for one
such tern.
In this report an attempt also has been mace to establish a standard term-
inology which ill conforr as mtch as possible to the conmon international us-
age of Conmunist terms which are employed by Communist Parties in countries
all over the world. The use of this articls as a reference work for official
standard organizational torminology of the JCP should contribute to a greater
clarification and ease of comprehension of all articles and reports pertaining
to the JCP.

7.
Importance of a Standard JCP Terminology
1. Introduction:
The organizational complexity of the Japan Communist Party (JCP) has
produced a wide variation of terminology peculiar only to the JCP. Added
to this large number of new terms employed by the Communist Party is the
necessity for translating these terms into English, This article is de-
signed to establish a standard terminology with the Romaji (Japanese writ-
ten in Roman letters) translations which will end the present confusion
concerning terms used by the JCF and to standardize their usage.
To facilitate the use of this article as a reference work, the defini-
tions of terms have been placed under major classifications, The standard
Romaji names and translations of the most important Party terms are listed
with brief explanations. A basic chart, "Comnand Echelons of the Japan
Communist Party" is presented (page SPA - ) to serve as an aid in finding
the Romaji and English translations of JCP organizational terminology. The
standard JCP terms in this article may have other good translations but for
the sake of uniformity, these standard terms are recommended.
The most frequently used mis-translations of JCP terms are described
in order to emphasize the undesirability of the use of such terms, The
standard terminology established in an appendix to the CIS Periodical Sum-
mary No. 34, 15 Nov 48, entitled "Alphabetical Index of Leftist Organiza-
tions in Japan" is not repeated in this article and should be consulted for
that phase of Communist activity.
2. Echelons of the Japan Communist Party:
a. Central Committee (Chuo Iinkai): The Central Committee of the JCP
is the national organ charged with the implementation of policies decided
by the National Congress or National Convention. This committee also formu-
lates and executes all necessary policy between the sessions of the National
Congress. All members of the Central Comnittee are publicly announced and
it is incorrect to refer to all representatives from the JCP Central Head-
quarters as Central Committee members.
b. Regional Committee (Chiho Iinkai): A Regional Committee of the JCP
embraces several Prefectural Committees and can be considered to be opera-
tional in scope rather than administrative. Regional Committees were first
officially authorized by the Sixth National Congress of the JCP in Japan.
The Party Constitution provides that the head of a Regional Conmittee will
be elected by the Regional Committee but at present each of the chiefs of
the Regional Committees is a Central Committee Organizer who is a member of
either the Central Committee or of the Central Committee Candidate Pool.
Eaeh Regional Committee has a Standing Committee (Jonin Iinkai) which exer-
cises the power of the Regional Committee when this committee is not meeting.
Some of the most common misnomers for the Regional Committee are: District
Committee, Great District Committee or Large District Committee.

Candidate is selected for that position.

5. Publications end Documents of the JCP:
a. Directives (Shirei) and Notifications (Tsutatsu): The Secretariat
normally issues all Party orders in the form of Directives or Notifications
although the Political Bureau and the Control Conmittee occasionally pub-
lish Directives and Notifications. The majority of these orders are num-
bered serially to facilitate filing but at times are published without num-
bers. These terms of Directives and Notifications are to be used instead
of Instruction or Notice.
b. Organization and Propaganda Guide (Soshiki Senden Shishin): The
Organization and Propaganda Guide is a joint publication of the Organization
and Activities Bureau and the Propaganda and Education Departments. It ap-
pears irregularly every 10 or 15 days and is considered to be a registered
and classified document by the JCF.
c. Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin (Soshiki Katsudo
Sokuho): This bulletin is published by the Organization and Activities.
Bureau at weekly intervals at present. It is a relatively new publication
which contains news and irs tructions concerning the pending Communist
"struggles." The Organization and Activity Emergency Bulletin is reported-
ly another JCP registered document.
d. Guide to Party Activity (To Katsudo Shishin): The Propaganda and
Education Department of the JCP headquarters publishes the Guide to Party
Activity. This guide is a new Party publication and is a registered JCP
document. It is reportedly published to supplement the Organization and
Propaganda Guide.
e. Official Party Publications: (For detailed information on JCP
publications see CIS Periodical Summary No. 31, 15 Aug 48, Page SUM - 23)
(1) Akahata (Red Flag): The official daily newspaper organ of
the JCP is Akahata.
This newspaper is published for both public and Party
member consumption.
The term Akahata is generally used without the trans-
lation.
(2) Zenei (Vanguard): Zenei is the official monthly publication
of the Communist Party and is for public consumptio.. Similarly to Akahata,
the term Zenei is usually used without the translation.
(3) Other Farty Organs and Publications: All echelons of the JCP
publish organ papers for education of members and public propaganda. Trans-
lation of these titles will normally produce standard and easily understood
names.
Central Headquarters Departments of the JCP also publish a large number
of pamphlets and leaflets vhich are prepared for public newsstand distribu-
tion for the purpose of propaganda and fund raising campaigns. Most of
these publications are issued by the Publications Department of the JCP but
other Central Headquarters Departments also issue such publications.

(1) National Congress or National Conrent
regular JCP National Congress or National Convention mus
once each year according to Party regulations. Emerg
one-half of the delegates to the previous National Congreso mo. d
National Congress is the highest policy-making body of the JCp and
ered to decide basic policies which continue in effect until the next
of the Congress. It also selects members of the Central Committee, the C
tral Committee Candidate Pool and the Control Committee. The National Gone
gress is attended by members of the Central Committee, the Central Committee
Candidate Pool and the Control Committee together with representatives from
the Prefectural Committees. National Congress is preferred, rather than Na-
tional Convention.
(2) National Conference (Zenkoku Kaigi): The Central Committee of
the JCP headquarters has the authority to call a National Conference to dis-
cuss urgent Party policies and issues which may arise in between sessions of
the National Congress. A National Conference is composed of the Central Com-
mittee plus representatives selected by the Prefectural Committees, The Na-
tional Conference has the authority to remove up to one-fifth of the total
membership of the Central Committee for cause, to elevate members of the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool to fill the vacancies created in the Central
Committee created by expulsion of undesirable members and then to select addi-
tional members for the Ca:didate Pool to fill the vacancies in that group.
(3) General Meeting (Sokai): The regulations of the JCP provide
that a General Meeting will be held at least once every four months. This
General Meeting is composed of the Central Committee plus members of the Cen-
tral Committee Candidate Pool. The meeting has the power to select members
for the Political Bureau and for the Secretariat from among the Central Com-
mittee membership and also conducts discussions on national strategy and
tactics of the Party.
(4) Central Committee Meeting (Chuo Iinkai): The Central Committee
is required to hold regular meetings at least once a month according to JCP
regulations. At these meetin s decisions are made relative to routine Party
affairs but due to the presence of many influential members on the committee,
policy decisions of great importance may be decided at regular Central Commit-
tee Meeti.gs.
b. Regional Level:
(1) Regional Party Conference (Chiho To Kaigi): The supreme policy-
making body on the regional level is the Regional Farty Conference. This con-
ference is attended by members of the Regional Committee, the Regional Commit-
tee Candidate Pool, representatives of the Prefectural Committees within the
region and representatives of the Central Committee. The Regional Party Con-
ference establishes plans based on the policies of the Central Committee and
selects members of the Regional Conmittee and the Regional Committee Candidate
Pool. Held once each year, the Regional Party Co. ference may be called into
emergency session upon direction of the Regional Conmittee or by request of
more than one-third of the Prefectural Committees.
(2) Regional Committee General Meeting (Chiho Iin Sokai): A Regional
Committee General Meeting is held at least once every three months and is com-
posed of members of the Regional Committee and the Regional Committee Candidate
Pool. The General Meeting has the authority to fill vacancies in the Regional
Committee by selecting replacements from the Regional Committec Candidate Pool.
All such selections are subject to approval by the Central Committee.

Committee. Meetings of Cell Groups and Cells aro held as needed but are
usually convened at re ular intervals. Fractions or Group Meetinss normally
termed as Gurupu Kaigi are held when their business dictates. A frequent mis-
translation for this torm is Group Council.
7. Importance of a Standard JCF Terminology:
The adoption of a standard organizational terminology for the Japan Com-
munist Party has many advantagcs and a definite importance. First, in order
to increase the comprehensibility of intelligence reports, a fixed and uniform
method of reporting Communist terms becomes necessary. At times a well prepared
report is rendered less valuable by confusing and erroneous Party terms which
are only understood by the persor pr-paring the report. An added difficulty
is the fact that the Japanese language has produccd a wide variation of names
for Communist terms so that there ma be stveral possible translations for one
such tern.
In this report an attempt also has been made to establish a standard term-
inology which ill conform as much as possible to the cotmon iuternational us-
age of Conmunist terms which are employed by Communict Parties in countries
all over the world. The use of this article as a reforence work for official
standard organizational terminology of the JCP should contribute to a reater
clarification and ease of comprehension of all articles and reports pertaining
to the JCP.

7. Impcrtance of a Standard JCP Terminology
1.
Introduction:
The crganisational complexity of the Japan Communist Party (JCp) has
produced & w.de variation of terminology peculiar only to the JCP. Added
to this large imber of new terms employed by the Communist Party is the
necessity foi trunslating these terms into English. This article is de
signed to estabiish a standard terminology with the Romaji (Japanese writ-
ten in Roman letters) translations which vill end the present confusion
conceming terns used by the JCF and to standardize their usage.
To facilitate the use of this article as a reference work, the defini-
tions of terms have been placed under major classifications. The standard
Romaji names and translations of the most important Party terms are listed
with brief explanations. A basic chart, "Comnand Echelons of the Japan
Communist Party" is presented (page SPA - ) to serve as an aid in finding
the Romaji and English translations of JCP organizational terminology. The
standard JCP terms in this article may have other good translations but for
the sake of uniformity, these standard terms are recommended,
The most frequently used mis-translations of JCP terms are described
in order to emphasize the undesirability of the use of such terms. The
standard terminology established in an appendix to the CIS Periodical Sum-
mary No. 34, 15 Nov 48, entitled "Alphabetical Index of Leftist Organiza-
tions in Japan" is not repeated in this article and should be consuited for
that phase of Communist activity.
2. Echelons of the Japan Communist Party:
a. Central Committee (Chuo Iinkai): The Central Committee of the JCP
is the national organ charged with the implementation of policies decided
by the National Congress or National Convention. This committee also formu-
lates and executes all necessary policy between the sessions of the National
Congress. All members of the Central Comaittee are publicly announced and
it is incorrect to refer to all representatives from the JCP Central Head-
quarters as Central Committee members.
b. Regional Committee (Chiho Iinkai): A Re ional Committee of the JCP
embraces several f efectural Committees and can be considered to be opera-
tional in scope ravher than administrative. Regional Committees were first
officially autacrized by the Sixth National Congress of the JCP in Japan.
The Party Constitution provides that the head of a Regional Committee will
be elected by the Regional Committee but at present each of the chiefs of
the Regional Committees is a Central Committee Organizer who is a member of
either the Central Committee or of the Central Conunittee Candidate Pool.
Each Regional Committee has a Standing Committee (Jonin Iinkai) which exer-
cises the power of the Regional Comnittee when this committee is not meeting.
Some of the most comnon misnomers for the Regional Committee are: District
Committee, Great District Conunittee or Large District Committee.
Note:
The basic reference for this article is "Guide to Communist
Terminclogy," 11 Feb 49, prepared by the Fourth CIC District.
Refererces also have been made to CIS files for the purpose
of checking and making amplifications.

standard Prefectural Committees in the rest of Japan.
c. Prefectural Committee (Ken Iinkai): The Prefectural Committ

or Prefectural Council. The Chairman of a Prefectural Committee shouldinot
be referred to as Chief Comnunist or leading Communist.

d. District Committee (Chiku Tinkai): The immediate supervisory organ
over Cells or Squads is known as the District Committee. This Committee is
responsible for the direction of local propaganda activities and for insur-
ing the participation of all subordinate organs in the various Party campaigns:
The person in charge of the District Committee is called a Chair an. Frequent
errors used in reporting on this Committee include: Regional Committee,
Branch of a Prefectural Committee or Area Committee.

Cell Group (Saibo Gun): The Cell Group or Squad Gra
standard JCp schelon but is often organized when there are thre
Cells or Squads closely together and joint control of the indiv
activities is desirable. A Cell Group is authorised to have a Cal
or Squad Group Committee. Cell Groups are often misnamed Joint Calls or
Cell Group Squads.
f. Cell (Saibo) or Squad (Han): The lowest official echelon of the
Communist Party is the Cell or Squad. At the Sixth National Convention of
the JCP it was decided that the name Cell would be changed to Squad for the
purpose of creating a better public impression by avoiding the use of the
intermationally used communistic term of Cell. Recently, the Party has re-
verted to the use of Cell and both terms are in common usage. A Cell is
formed with a minimun of three members who are living or working close
enough for mutuel. ccmmunicacion. No maximum size has been esteblished for
Cells and some Colis report as many as 80 members. A coanon crroneous
design. tion for Ce!ls is Local Franch.
g Fraction (Furaku) (Written in Katakana): The mos confusing of
Communist terms ar the words Fiaction and Faction. A Fraction is an. im-
portant Party tern enc means a group of JCP members vho may also be organ-
ized into one or more cells ho are placed into or recruited. from a target
organization over which the Party wishes to establish control. Thus, a
Fraction is the unit of infiltration of the Party. In Japan, a Fraction
may be either revealed or it may cperate uncer cover. In Japanese labor
unions, there are instances of both a concealed and open Fraction in the
same union. An open Fraction generally operates under the name of Manage-
ment Cell and issues orders and publications in the name of the Cell. A
Fraction is not an actual echelon in the Party organization but can be at-
tached to the formal structure at any level. In certain large target
groups such as the Japan Farmers Union, the Fractions are pyramided into.
echelons with the various levels of the echelon maintaining liaison and
contact with the corresponding echelon of the Party. The-revised term for
Fraction is Group (Gurupu) (Written in Katakana) but Fraction still re-
mains in general use.
The word Faction has no particular meaning in Communist terminology
and it has the normal meaning of two or more groups with opposing ideas in'"
the same organization. This term is used in many types of organizations,
both Communist and non-Communist. Followers of the several cliques in the.
JCP or partisans of certain leaders are properly known as Factions, such
as the Yenan Faction, the Nosaka Faction or the Prison Faction.
3. Communist Position Titlcs:
a. Secretary General (Shoki Cho): The standard international Commu-
nist term for the executive head of the Party within each country is Secre-
tary General. Chicf Clerk or Head Clerk are hot correct interpretations of
this position title.
b. Members of Committees: The best usage in referring to the posi-
tions of members of any Conmunist Committee is to say member of the Central
Committee, Kanto Regiona.. Jonnitteeman or member of the Control Committee.
The use of such terms as Prefectural Executive or Distict Director for mem-
bers of committees is erroncous and confusing.
c. Organizers or Ors (Orugu) (Written in Katakana): The term Organi-
zer refers to a specific position in the Communist Party and certain persons
are designated as Organizers. Although every Party member has thc duty of
recruiting new members and organizing new groups, they are not officially
considered as Organizers and every Communist agitator should not be referred
to as an Organizer. Organizers are normally officially assigned to echelons

level.
4. Central Headquarters Departments and Bureaus:
The Departments and Bureaus of the JCP Central Headqua
duplicated in the lower echelon headquarters of the. Party. There is no
fixed organization of these Departments and Bareaus in the Varibus Reglonal
Prefectural or District headquarters and they"are established when the local
situation makes them necessary. The same names and translations are general-
ly usea.
a. Secretariat (Shoki Kyoku): The central administrative agenoy for
the JCP is the Secretariat. The main duties of the Secretariat are to issue
official Directives and Notifications in the name of the Certral Committee;
to keep official statistics on the internal affairs of the Party; and to pro-
vide an accounting of finances on a national basis. most frequently the
Secretariat is misnamed the Clerk Bureau, Clerical Bureau, Clerk Section,
Secretary Department of Secretary's Pool.
b. Political Bureau or Politburo (Seiji Kyoku): The Political Bureau
is the Party agency authorized to decide general and political policies for
the Party's activities. These decisions are subject to a theoretical re
view and approval by the Centrel Committee when in session. The Political
Bureau has been misnamed the Political Board, the Political Affairs. Section
and the Political Department.
c. Organization and Activities Bureau (Soshiki Katsudo Shido Bu): The
Organization and Activities Bureau of Central Headguarters makes general
daily decisions on tactical measures and also aids in the formulation of
propa anda keynotes and tactical guides. Although other translations of
this term may be correct, the title of Organization and Activities Bureau
best describes this agency. Incorrect translations are Organization Action
and Leadership Department and the Formation and Activity Guidance Section.
d. Control Committee (Tosei Iinkai): The Control Commi tee of the JCP
Central Headquarters is the watchdog of the headquarters. Tts duties in-
clude inspection and supervision of the implementation of decisions of-the
National Congress and the Central Committee, enforcement of Party regula-
tions and defense against anti-Communist elements who seek to destroy the
Party. The Committee holds regular general meetings at least once a month
and conducts joint meetings with the Political Bureau when important urgent
matters arise. It is composed of not more than 10 members who are elected
by the National Congress.
e. Central Committee Candidate Pool (Chuo Iin Koho): Members of the
Central Committee Candidate Pool are elected by the JCP National Congress.
Provisions are made for a total of 25 persons to be held in this pool and
whenever a vacancy occurs in the Central Committee, a Central Committee
Candidate is selected for that position.

ogy of these departments usually is the same as for the Central Headguarters'
Departments.
d. District Level: The highest body of the JCP Districts is the Dis-
trict Party Conference (Chiku To Kaigi) which mests twice each year by order
of the District Committee. Special sessions may be called by either the di-
rection of the District Comnittee or by request of over onc-third of the Party
members under the jurisdiction of the District. In between meetings of the
District Party Conferencc, business is carried on by meetings of the District
Comnittee. Meetings of Cell Groups and Cells are held as needed but are
usually convened at re ular intervals. Fractions or Group Heetinss normally
termed as Gurupu Kaigi are held when their businoss dictates. A frequent mis-
translation for this torm is Group Council.
7. Importance of a Stan dard JCF Terminology:
The adoption of a standard organizational terminology for the Japan Com-
munist Party has many advantages and a definite importance. First, in order
to increase the comprehensibility of intelligence reports, a fixed and uniform
method of reporting Communist terms becomes necessary. At times a well prepared
report is rendered less valuable by confusing and erroneous Party terms which
are only understood by the person preparing the report. An added difficulty
is the fact that the Japanese language has produced a wide variation of names
for Communist terms so that there ma, be several possible translations for one
such tern.
In this report an attempt also has been made to establish a standard term-
inology which ill conforr as uch as possible to the conmon international us-
age of Conmunist terms which are employed by Communist Parties in countries
all over the world. The use of this article as a reference work for official
standard organizational terminology of the JCP should contribute to a greater
clarification and ease of comprehension of all articles and reports pertainin
to the JCP.

SUBJECT: Future of the Social Democratic Party.

Mrs. Jane M ALDEN
YOMELUBO Mitsusuke, Socialist Party member of the
House of Representatives is associated with the center
faction of the Party. He was formerly Minister of Labor
in the Katayama Cabinet, and for thirty years has been
associated with the Sea en's Union.. In the prewar
period he was a member of the Shakai Taishuto (Social
Mass Party) predecessor of the present Socislist Party.
Mr Yonekubo expressed his views on conditions within
the Socialist Party its relations with labor and farmer
groups, and the party's prospects for the future.
In Mir Yonekubo's cpinion, the Socialist Party will
probably split into two roups within the next year or
two. He did not think that the split would come earlier
than this because the left wing and its lebor support
would attempt to work from within t. e party for the time
being He personally would sontinue to work to avoid
but he was not hopeful that party
a split in
unity could be mainteined. He himself would go with the
left wing of the party in the event the party split into
two groups
He has refrained to date from associating
himself with the left wing because of his desire to
conciliate the differences between the right and left
within the party. lthough Mr. Yonekubo did not say so,
he implied that his decision to throw in his lot with
the left wing in the event of a split was a recent
decision, and that for a long time he had remained
undecided on a course of action He believes that the
left wing is firmly anti-cormunist, and that is is
increasing

Party Organi
MEMORANDUM
May 31, 1949。
Subject: Local Crganization and Activities of the
Democratic Liberal, Democratic, and Socialist
Parties.
The Democrotic Liberal and Demooratic Parties on a
local level are pharacterized by a lack of struotural
organization, the absence of initiative on the part of
the local leadershir and an almost complete dependence
on and orientution toward national party headquarters.
At the same time there is very little interest in looal
problems or local political activity. The strength of
the party is measured in terms of the number of repre-
sentatives in the Diet, not in terms of the lodal
political situation sic the local leadership has no
conception that the party as a whole might be in need
of or could utilize è strong local base. To a limited
extent the recent Comunist suecesses in both the
House of Represeatatives and local by-eleetions. have
stimuleted local perty leaders to think in terms of
altering their trud' pional concep of local party
organization and assiviuy but thr are not taking the
initiative and are wai sing instead direction from
national perty readpeerters.
The losal rgunization of the conservative parties
exists primarily en poper. As a general rule the member-
ship is small, ie vding individuals who register their
membership with the party and a few party leaders. There
are no mebership es. no party newspapers, newssheets
or the like, and no party meetings open to the general
membership. In s ort an crdinary citizen who is
interested in participating in political activity or in
commw ffairs would not find any inoentive to join
either of the cons ervative parties. The strength of the
conservative parties lies in a personal type of party
organization whiien depends on the influence and position
of an individual leader in the commit   ho has a
personal following. This individual leader may in turn
belong to one of the parties, but his followers belong
to him rather than to the party
The
RESTRICTED

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.98)
The looal branches of the honservat
look to the party headquarters for dir
of both party policies and party organization
suggestion that the local chapters might benetit
more independenoe in dealing with local problema
countered by one party leader with the statement that
since the political and sconomic life of the country wa
centralized in Tokyo it would be of no advantage to
decentralize party organization. The local chapters
Beek approval from Tokyo for the organization of their.
local branch, and any changes in the paper organization
are first approved by the national headquarters.  Candidates
for the Diet are recommended by the local chapter and
approved by the headquarters. Even candidates for
important prefectural offices, such as governors, mayors
of large cities, end even candidates for prefectural
assemblies are  ently approved by party headquarters
In the case of both Diet members and local executives
the rarty headquarters has felt free to act against the
recomiendations of the local chapter.
This centralized control over the activities of the
local branches is not carried out by a well organized
party machine. The only link between the local and
national orgenizations for all practical purposes is the
party's prefectural representatives in the Diet. The
Diet members are responsible for conveying the policies
and plans of the national heedquarters to the local branches
and conversely for representing the local chapters at
national headquarters. This method of maintaining party
organization is enti ely cependent upon the individual
Diet member, who srends most of Lis time in Tokyo, and
who may or may no: be an effective leader of the local
branch. In effeet the local branches spend most of
their time waiting for their Diet members to return from
Tokyo to provide Teacerslin cna direotion.  When a pre-
feotural branch oes not have representatives in the Diet,
it is virtually cut cff from party headquarters and is
severely handicapyed in rebuilding its local strength.
The party headquarters does not give any financial
assistanoe to its local chapters which must finance all
local aotivities cus of their own sources of revenue.
The ohapters of the conservative parties do not as a rule
colleot dues from their members and their only regular
source of income is an assessment on their representatives
in the prefecturel and municipal assemblies. In a few
cases a party's Diet members also contribute regularly
to the local ohaptei, but in general Diet members make
a fixed contribution to the nationel party treasury
Private contributions to the local branch represent the
main source of income. The local branches do not give
financial support to their candidates, and in at least
one

cated, that it imposes unnecessary burdens and limitations
on both candidates and parties, and that the public
management of campaigns is too costly to the local adminis-

CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.96)
Opinions af Lananase Dlection Offiaiala and Leadars
of the Maior Palitical Parties agarding the Dectien 
The prefectures covered in this report are Iyoto, Hyoge.
Hiroshima, Shimane and Osaka. Separate discussions were held
with representatives of the prefectural election committees,
and officials of the local government concerned with electiens,
and with leaders of the four major political parties, the
Democratic Liberals, the Democrats, the Socialists and the
Communists. This report represents a consensus of the views
expressed by these leaders.
SUMOLARY
Discussions with election officials and party leaders
led to the following general conclusions: 1. the election
campaign law did not provide for adequate publicity for the
campaign and as a result the voters did not have available
to them sufficient information on candidates or issues;
2. the responsibility for the supervision of the campaie
rested primarily with the local government officials rather
than with the prefectural election committee; 3. the financial
burden on prefectural and local governments to supplement the
funds provided by the national government accentuated the
already critical problem of local government finances; 4. the
campaign law in effect made it difficult for political parties
to be of any assistance to their candidates, the burden of the
campaign resting on the candidate himself, thus further emplhasising
the izportance of the candidate as opposed to the party: 5. there
was ge oral agreement that a check-off ballot was administratively
feasilie and also that it was desirable; 6. there was no agree-
ment on changes in the sise of the electoral districts or the
representation from each district.
Carmaisn Speachan.
The eleotion campaign law provided for three types of
campaign speeches - competitive speech meatings, private speech
meetings and street speeches. As regarås competitive spesches
the political party leaders and election officials approved of
this campaign medium, but suggested that the number of such
speeches should be increased in order that the candidate could
reach a larger number of voters in the district and that the
COHFIDNTIAL

=== Page 354 === (confidence 0.98)
tima for the individl
mit a faller epressien of
pelitical leaders did mot think that the
helped their candidates, but they canceded
of this type of mesting warrented an inorease
that the andience was cemposed largely of partisem suppertere
who heckled the opposing speakers and applanded their omm cam
didate creating disorder and giving the impressiem to the
audience in general that the particular candidate had a large
popular following.
The provision of the law rolating to the private speech
meeting drew the most criticiam from the various party leaders
and officials interviewed. There was no party line on the
suggested changes in the regulations regarding private speech
meetings. The majority of those interviewed, regardless of
party affiliation, favored an increase in the number of private
speech meetings authoriged, and a number of leaders supported
the proposal that the number of private meetings should be un-
limited. A minority (the Democrats in Kyoto, the Democratie
Liberals in Hyoto and the Socialists in Hiroshima) favored the
elimination of all private speech meetings. There was agreement
that there was not adequate publicity by the election committees
as to the time and place of such meetings. In one prefecture
(Hyogo).for example, the committee put up only an average of
10 posters announcing the holding of each private speech meeting.
In addition the parties complained that the posters drafted by
the election committee lacked imagination and were too much alike.
The parties and officials were in favor of turning over to the
political parties and candidates the responsibility for making
their own arrangements for private speech meetings and all the
publicity attendent upon such meetings. One suggestion was made
to the effect that the parties should be responsible for all the
arrangements for private meetings with the election committee
continuing to pay the expenses. The selection of the place for
the private meetings by the committee was the subject of
universal criticiam. The committees selected public buildings.
usually schools, which were often located in inaccessable places
or in areas away from the center of a party's potential audience.
There was general agreement that private speech meetings should
not require the presence of the candidates, nor for that matter
should street speeches require a candidate to be present.
The provisions in the election campaign law relating to
the number of times a representative of a candidate could speak
for the candidate were the subject of considerable criticiem by
the various party leaders and election officials. There was
goneral agreement that the representatives of a candidate
should be permitted to speak more often without his presence.
It was obvious that in some cases this criticism stemmed from
the poor showing made by some candidates in speech meetings and
the fact that the party would have been able to provide more
effectire speakers. In other cases the criticism was based on
the fact that if more people had been able to deliver speeches,
without the presence of the candidate, more voters would have been
reached and the electorate would have been better acquainted with
the policies of the various parties. In addition, as one
Democratic Party official s tated, speeches are the principle
weapon of a candidate and a party that lacks extensive financial
support.

In actual fact the secretariat ran the elections in mest

formulation of policy leaving the implementation  te the secretariat.
Where the committee was at all active the question of the budget-
ing and expending of election funds was a bone of contention
between.the committee and the local government as represented by
the committee secretariat. In two prefectures the election
committee complained that it had no control over the financial
aspects of the election, that the committee was ignored in making
the original estimates of funds needed for the election, that the
committee was without influence in determining how the funds re-
ceived from the national government should be expended, and that
in actual fact the suggestions of the committee on such matters
as the putting out of publicity materials concerning the election
were rejected by the local government officials on the basis of
insufficient funds. One Blection Committee chairman pointed out
that if it is the intention of the law to estahlish an election
committee to supervise the campaign and the election which is
independent of the local and national government, then it is
essential that the committee have a role in the determination of
how the election funds are to be spent.
Another aspect of the financial problem is that of local
governments supplementing the funds allotted by the national govem-
ment for the election. In all cases there was a complaint that
not enough money was provided for the election and in all c ases
the money spent on the election exceeded the amount received from
the national government. Whether or not this was primarily due
to inadequate funds or whether it should be attributed to poor
management on the part of local election officials is not immediate-
ly evident. It would appear. however, that there was not sufficient
money allocated by the national government to provide adequate
campaign services. In the case of a prefecture like Osaka, the

Party leaders and election officials showed considerable
understanding, of, even if they did not always agree with, the
basis need for economizing in election expenditures both in terms
of public funds expended and the amount of money a candidate and
party were permitted to spend on the election. On the other hand.
it was pointed out that there is a point beyond which a cheap.
election is in fact a delusion. Too drastic limitations on
campaign expenditures, whether public or private funds, tends to
encourage rather than discourage the spending of money outside
of the provisions of the law. On the basis of the information
obtained in the prefectures visited, it appears that the majority
of the candidates spent considerably more then the legal limit.
and that because outlets for the spending of money were aeverely
limited this money went into channels which did not afford the
voters as a whole any opportunity to learn more about candidates
and parties. For example, parties and candidates could not put
up posters, distribute handbills, etc., where they might actually
exceed their authorized campaign expenditures by putting out very
elaborate campaign materials, or by distributing more than the
quantity permitted. If this had been the case, as it has been
in past elections, the voter would at least have received some
bensfits from the increased publicity. Instead as the campaign
progressed it became obvious to most candidates that they were
not getting adequate publicity through the authorized channels
and therefore they sought to increase their chances of being
elected by distributing money where it would do the most good
and would be the least conspicuous. It is recogmized, of course.
that bribery in any one of many forms has been prevalent in
past Japanese elections and that it is very difficult to control
this type of activity. but at the same time it is possible that the
absence of sufficient outlets for campaign propaganda made the
distribution of money more of a necessity than usual.
Funda for tha Public Managed Campaign.
The problems of providing more funds for the public managed
election campaign was a subject of frequent discussion with
party leaders and election officials. The easiest and most
common solution was to suggest that the national government put
up more money for the election. Barring this possibility, and
assuming the need for more funds for election campaign there was
a division of opinion as to whether the candidates should be re-
quired to give more money to the election committee for their
campaign, or whether the public managed campaign should be limited
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 357 === (confidence 0.99)
in scepe and, the parties and candidates allowed te
more freely in the campaign. All groups realised thatt
more money from each candidate would eliminate memy
from the competition. One interesting suggestion was
by the election officials in Osaka. They suggested that each
candidate be required to put up a total of 50,000 yen for his
public managed campaign and that the guarantee bond as such be
eliminated. At the present time the candidate is required te put
up 20,000 yen for his public managed campaign and 30, 000 yen a
a guarantee bond, but the 30, 000 yen is returned to him after the
election if he receives more than a specified number of votes.
While the deposit of 50,000 yen with the election.committee for
the campaign would be in reality an increase in the amount of
money the candidate was required to furnish for his campaign.
since no portion of it would be returnable, the suggestion that
the guarantee bond is no longer necessary is worth consideration.
It was the cont ention of the election officials that the purpose
of the guarantee bond, which was originally instituted to dis-
courage irresponsible candidates with no chance of being elected
from competing. was in effect accomplished by the requirement that
a candidate contribute 20,000 yen to the government for his public
managed campaign.
Political Party Activity in the Campaign.
In almost all cases the political party leaders stated that
their party was unable to assist its candidates in the election
campaign even to the extent they had assisted them in previous
years. The Communist Party constituted the main erception to this,
although even this party desired a relaxation of the restrictions
on party activities. The only other exception was in the case
of the Democratic Liberal Party in two prefectures where the party
organization had been strengthened as compared to 1947 when it
was extremely weak.
With the exception of the Communists, political perties do
not, and have never, rendered any financial assistance to their
candidates to meet campaign expenses. In the past the parties
have been moderately active in assisting their candidates by
providing speakers, holding party rallies, putting out general
party publicity, and similar activities. Bither as a result of
the provisions of the campaign law. or as a result of the inter
pretation of these provisions, the parties were for the most part
inactive in the campaign. In the past the elections have provided
an occasional and much needed stimulus to party organisation,
and serve as a means of building up party organisation. Moreover.
the Japanese voter is inclined to consider the candidate more
important than the party. and the absence of party activity in
this election campaign only served to reinforce the importance of
the candidate as an individual and the relative mimportance of the
party in the minds of the voter.

House-to-house canvassing of voters was generally disapproved,

Check-aff Ballot.
The election officials, with the exception of those in
Shimane, favored the introduction of a check-off ballot, that
is a ballot which'lists the candidates names and requires the
voter only to mk the name of the candidate he wishes to vote
for. The election officials considered that this type of ballet
would reduce the number of invalid ballots, would make ballot
counting easier, and would make it easier for the voter to cast
his ballot. While the Shimane election officials agreed that a
check-off ballot would reduce the number of invalid votes they
contended that the people would not underetand how to use this
type of wailot, whereas they had had long experience with the
write in ballot. All of the election officials agreed that it
would be administratively possible to print up the ballots and
distribute them prior to the election providing the period during
which a candidate could register his candidacy was reduced to
about the first five days of the campaign. The eleotion officials
pointed out that in actual practice most candidates register their
candidacy during the opening days of the campaign. Some questions
were raised as to how to meet such problems as the withdrawal of
candidates and the changes in party affiliation of candidates
after the ballots had been printed. As to the withdrawal of
candidates the election officials thought that the only way to
meet this problem was to delete the candidates name from all the
ballots, but at the same time they suggested that no refunds of
guarantee bond or campaign funds deposited with the committee
be made for any reason after a candidate has notified the
committee of his candidacy. The second problem that of the
changes in party affiliation of candidates after registration was
more difficult to solve. The best suggestion made was that the
responsibility for designation of official party candidates
rest with the parties and not with the candidates. In this way
the various officials thought there would be fewer cases of
changes in party affiliation.

The Charge d'Affaires ad interim has the honor to enclose here-
with two copies of a "Report of Surveillance" dated May 28, 1949.
covering the local general and by-elections held in Japan during the
period from January 1 to May 1, 1949, as prepared by the Legal and
Government Division of the Military Government Section of Eighth
Army Headquarters.
The report, which relates only to local elections for the period
mentioned, covers 21o by-elections and 418 general elections, or a
total of 628. It is of interest to note that there were only 2,947
candidates for 2,055 offices, or about 44% more candidates than va-
cancies to be filled. The report also draws out the fact that polit-
ical parties have had little influence in the local elections, as the
voter normally casts his ballot for the person rather than for a polit-
ical party.

REPORT OF SURVEILLANCE
Reported Local General and By-Eleetions
1 January to 1 May 1949
1. Definition of Local Elections
Elections in Japen are of two types: the generel elections whether
netional or local and the by-elections whether netionel or local. The gen-
erel elections are those which are held in accordence with the laws for (1)
the regular election on the expiration of the incumbent's regular four-year
term of office, (2) vote of non-confidence ageinst the premier, governor or
mayor, (3) ection from recell petitions, which results in either the vacancy
of the offices of fovernor end mayor or in the dissolution of the assembly.
(4) death of the governor or mayor, end (5) resignation of the governor or
mayor. In Japan the term by-election hes a particuler definition. The by-
lections in Jepan are held for members of the National Diet, members of lo-
c:1 assemblies and members of the Agricultural Lend Commission and Agricul-
ril Adjustment Committee. They are held only to fill unexpired terms of
orfice. By-elections are provided for (1) members of the House of Represent-
atives in Article 79 end 80 of the Law for the Election of the Members of the
House of Representativec, (?) members of the House of Councillors in Article
71 and 72 of the Law for the Members of the House of Councillors, (3) members
of the Agriculturel Land Commission in the Agricultural Land Adjustment Lew
in Article 15, and (4) members of the Agricultural Ad iustment Committee in
Article 13, Cabinet Order No. 247 of 1948. By-elections for all local pub-
Lic bodies are provided for in Section VII in the Lew Concerning Locel Autonomy.
Both the general and the by-election cen be a no poll election. When
the number of candidates running equals the number of vecancies to be filled.
there is no contest end no vote is required.
II. Election Laws and Cabinet Orders
There are four lavs which provide for and govern the conduct of these
locel general and by-elections. Laws numbered 1, 3 and 3, listed below, pro-
vide a reason for having these elections and la's numbered 3 and 4 provide
the method and means for conducting them. The Cabinet Order for each law is-
sued in terms of each law gives imolementing deteils to aid in its administra-
tion.

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.99)
1. The Law for Elcction of Members of the House of Representatives in
Article 67 stetes, "pubiic'officials of the State end local public entities
who, in accordanco with the provisions.of law, ere prohibited from holding
concurrently the office of member of the House of Representative, shall not
(file notice of candidecy for the House of Representatives) until efter the
persons concerned have resigned the office of public officials."
2. The Netional Public Service Law in Article 102 states, "no person
in the service shall be e candidate for elective public office."
3. The Lew Concerning Locel Autonomy provides not only a reason for
heving elections but also the processes by which they cen be conducted. The.
Law Concerning Local Autonomy in Articles 57, 92. 127, 141 and 143 provides
the legal restreints ageinst chiefs or assemblymen of locel public bodies
from becoming Diet members concurrently. The time limit within which all of
the elections must be held is provided for in Article 24 es follows, "..
the elections of the assemblymen of en ordinery public body end the chief
thereof shall be held forthwith within 60 deys of the date on which reasons
have occurred for holding such elections......" Moreover, in Chapter IV the
legal procedure is established for holding ell locel generel and by-elections.
The no poll election for locel governments is provided for in Article 58
of the Lew Concerning Locel Autonomy. This Article is besed upon similar pro-
visions in the former three femous organic acts: The Lew Concerning the Or-
genization of Prefectures, the Law Concerning the Organization of Cities, end
the Lew Concerning the Orgenization of Towns end Villeges.
4. The Lew Concerning the Regulation of Politicel Contributions end Ex-
penditures, Law No. 194 es of 29 July 1948, is the corrupt practices act of
Japan which limits political contributions end expenditures end establishes
penalties for infractions.
III. Election Surveillence Progrem
On 1l Febru ry 1949, a commend lutter wes dispatched by Headquarters
Fighth United States Army to subordinate headauerters concerned indiceting
the generel end by-elections which vould probebly occur in their zones of
responsibility end requesting two reports for each election. The first re-
port was to be submitted immediately unon ennouncement of the locel or gen-
erel by-election, giving the neme end locetion of the vecency and the date of
the election. The second report to be submitted within fourteen deys follov-
ing the date of the election required more deteiled information, including en
evelustion of the results. A negetive report was requested for each prefec-
ture not holding any elections.
The extensive surveillance of polling pleces end ballot counting sta-
tions was not required as on 25 Jenuary 1949 for the netional general elec-
tions. On that occesion all the military government teems, together with
2

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.98)
the many other surveillence teams composed of troops from the teotical
under their supervision, were required to be in the field an extended p

VII. Conclusion
Local general and by-elcctions in addition to roflooting tho politi
cal tomper of the pooplo often indicetos: (1) the oxistonco and nature of
political, social, cconomic, and idcological probloms affocting the objec-
tives of the Oceupation, (2) the magnitude of the issucs as revoaled by the
trend of succcssive clection, (3) tho grounds for a proper courso of action
which military governm nt teams can pursuo locally, and (4) tho spccific
areas in which greatest attontion should bo focused.
Through careful analysis military government units can dctormino the
extent to which Occuyetion policics and thoir on activities are diroctly
or indirectly mado issues in the campaigns. By watching tho fortunos of
parties and organizutions hich on the one hand ondorse thom. and by obsorv-
ing tho fate of those which are highly critical of them on the other hand,
military covernment should be ablo to determino which areas or which pro-
grems should rec ive increcsed attention. Campaign criticism of unpopular
policies and programs should be studicd with a view through inercased dis-
sominetion of information, to clcar up popular misconcoptions and overcome
outmoded traditions or projudices; or lse it may be considored altogether
appropri to to urge revision or abandonmont in vhole or in part. For it
should be readily apparent that only thoso programs and policios which
stand a chence of gaining popular acceptance will tako root pormanontly;
all othurs, besides avakin antipathy tovards their would-be bonofactors.
vill be scuttled unccromoniously at the first opportunity. Military govern-
imont's comonts and ovaluations form tho backbono of all clcction reports,
without which bare statistics are often devoid of real meaning•
Asido from draving pertinent conclusions as to the significence of
local lcctions in t: ir arca of rosponsibility, vhich is porhaps the most
important reason for their surveillanee, military governnont can also do
much to cducate the Japanose in the ussent ials of intelligent politics. By
oncouraging tho Japanoso votors to fonm political partios to tako a moro
active stand in adhering to concrote issucs as vell as to compete actively
in every olection, and by oducating the people in tho methods of success-
ful eloction techniques, military govermnent teams are aiding the citizenry
to secure the desired maximum offective oxpression of the peoplo's will and
the assurance of the defeat of hichly organized pressure groups. This task
becomes a progrossivoly more important part of the mission of military gov-
ornment in the Occupation.

VIII. Rocommendations
1. It is ricommended that the systom of plural voting bo adoptod
in placo of tho highly rostrictive (singlo voting) systom now used. In
Japan today, the political situation far outmodos tho ono in which the
systom was ostablished. The systom was adopted to ensuro minority repre-
sontation which one-half century ago in Japan was to como from particular
rural arcas. Tho conditions of thoso years, not to say tho intervening
oncs, are far removed from those of today. Tho conduct of tho prossure
groups today vith their socially insidious tactics completcly nogates tho
offects desired by tho carly law makers.
2. Furtier, i: is reconmended that provisions of tho clcction laws.
bo changed in vhich incumbents of olcctive positions aro roquired to re-
sign prior to running for another officc. Needlcss cxpenditures now sad-
dled upon tho local public body concornod vould bo eliminated.

Subject: Background of Riots at Tokyo Metropolitan
Assembly, May 30-31, 1949.
844006-449
of June 4, 1949, concerning riots at the Tokyo Metropoli-
tan Assembly on May 30 and 31, 1949, as a consequence of
894.00/6-2349
communist-inspired opposition to new municipal regulations
governing parades and demonstrations, the Chargé d'Affaires
1/
ad interim has the honor to transmit a memorandum pre-
pared by Foreign Service Officer Richard H. LAMB giving
significant background information on this incident.
The information in the enclosed memorandum is
based upon discussions with a competent official of
General Headquarters and is supported in some respects
by information obtained from a Japanese Government
employee who was ordered to attend the demonstrations.
At the time of the Korean riots in the Osaka-Kobe
district in April, l948, local police authorities, on
instructions from the Commanding General, I Corps,
issued an order forbidding demonstrations not previously
authorized by the police and specifying that participants
in such demonstrations would be subject to arrest. That
ordinance was later rescinded because of its apparent
violation of Article 2l of the Constitution. Steps to
rescind the ordinance were presumably initiated by the
Government Section of General Headquarters, which at
the same time prepared a "model" Public Safety Ordinance
which was sent to the Commanding General, I Corps, with
the suggestion that it be used as a basis for future
municipal ordinances. The model ordinance reportedly
outlined procedures to govern the conduct of only "moving"
meetings, that is, parades.
On April 4, 1949, the Commanding General, I Corps,
is reported to have issued instructions to ali military
governnent teams concerning a new "model" ordinance
to be

prefectural authorities. The new model ordina
with both "moving" (parades) and "standing" (

suggested by the Commanding General, I Corps, is far
more restriotive than that proposed by the Government
Section, and the extension of its restrictive provisions
to "standing" demonstrations in effect prohibits any
spontaneous public meeting where it is impossible to
obtain necessary police aui norization seventy-two hours

subject to arrest
It is not certain if communist leaders have been

General, I Corps. It is also apparent that at the time
of the riotous demonstrations at the Tokyo Metropolitan
Assembly, the Communists were not aware of what attitude
the Assembly would take or what kind of draft ordinanoe
was likely to be proposed. From experience in other
cities, however, the Communists were convinced that a
rest ictive ordinance would come under disoussion, and
it was undoubtedly their intention, through the

MEMORANDUM
Subject: Background of the Demonstrations at the Tokyo
Metropolitan Assembly, May 30-31, 1949.
It is believed that the following information, which
was obtained from a competent SCAP official who is also
a close personal friend, and which was supported in some
respects by a Japanese Government employee who was ordered
to attend the demonstrations, may be of interest in
connection with the Communist-inspireddemonstrations
which took place at the Tokyo Metropolitan Assembly on
May 30-31, 1949, in protest against the new Public Safety
Ordinance for Tokyo which the Assembly was believed to
be considering.
At the time of last year's Korean demonstrations
in Osaka and Kobe, an ordinance was enacted on instruotions
from the Commanding General, I Corps, forbidding demonstra-
tions not previously authorized by the police, and specifying
that persons who participated in such demonstrations would
be subject to arrest. This ordinance, which appeared to
be in direct violation of Article 2l of the Constitution
and which did not seem to be justified on grounds of
military necessity, was considered to be entirely too harsh
and was soon rescinded on instructions from SCAP, presumably
at the instance of Government Section. At that time a
"model" Public Safety Ordinance was drafted by Government
Section and sent to I Corps, with the suggestion that it
be used as a basis for any ordinances regarding public
meetings which might be necessary in the future. The
essential points of the "model" ordinance were as follows:
(1) permits for mass meetings would be requested at least
72 hours in advance, and if no reply were received until
24 hours before the meeting was scheduled to be held, it
could be assumed that permission had been granted; (2)
permission would be refused only if there were a "clear
and present danger" to public peace, and in the event of
refusal, the municipal or prefectural assembly would be
immediately notified of the reasons for the refusal;
(3) persons organizing or otherwise responsible for holding
unauthorized meetings would be subject to arrest; (4) the
ordinance would apply only to "moving" meetings (parades, eto.)
and not to "standing" meetings.
Apparently
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
CONDIDENTIAL
Apparently no further major dir
mass meetings were issued by I Corpasuntil
1949, when Major-General Coulter, succesmort
General Swing as Commanding General, I Corpet
a letter (restrioted) to all military government
in I Corps area regarding public safety ordinancos
letter contained a new "model" ordinanoe, which militar
government officers were instructed to suggest to prer
authorities who might be considering suoh legislation.
General Coulter's "model" ordinance appears to have
differed from that prepared by Government Section in at
least two important respects: (1) it applied to "standing
as well as "moving" demonstrations; (2) not only the
organizers of unauthorized demonstrations, but all the
participants therein, would be subject to arrest. General
Coulter's suggestions, it appears, have been generally
accepted (or seized upon) by prefectural authorities, and
already ordinances have been enacted in a number of pre-
fectures which embody the two principles indicated above.
It need not be pointed out that the ordinanoe as
suggested by General Coulter is infinitely more restriotive
than that proposed by Government Section, and puts much
greater power in the hands of the police. The distinotion
betweer "moving" and "standing" meetings is important.
"Moving" meetings will certainly obstruct traffic and are
likely to cause serious disturbances, and it is olearly
necessary that the police be informed in advance so that
plans and preparations can be made. The extension of these
provisions to "standing" meetings, however, in effect
prohibits all spontaneous public meetings, in whioh it
will often be impossible to obtain the necessary police
authorization seventy-two hours in advance. The provision
which makes participants as well as organizers subject
to arrest is even more restrictive: it means that anyone
who attends such a meeting, including the "man on the
street" who happens to walk by and decides to stop and.
listen, may be legally placed under arrest by the police.
Although curiously enough the Communists have not yet
raised the issue, these two provisions to a large extent
nullify the guarantee of freedom of assembly contained
in the Constitution.
With these facts in mind the motives of the Communists
in organizing the demonstrations at the Tokyo Metropolitan
Assembly may perhaps be better understood. There is not
the slightest doubt that these meetings were Communist-
organized: in at least one Government Kodan the union
leaders, who are acknowledged Communists, "ordered" all
but a necessary minimum of employees to leave work and
attend the meetings, without telling them what their
purpose was to be. Even after the demonstrations were
over
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
over many, if not most, of the demonstrators did 
know what they were opposing.
It 1s not certain how muoh of the above informat
regarding Government Section's ordinance and the I Corl
version is known to Communist leaders. In any event.
they could not have known what the attitude of the Asmombly
was to be, since at the time of the demonstrations the
Assembly had not yet begun to deliberate on the subjeot;
and, as will be apparent from what follows, they could
not have known what sort of draft ordinanee was likely
to be proposed to the Assembly. What they did know,
however, was that highly restrictive ordinanoes had
recently been enacted in some prefectures, and that the
Tokyo Assemblymen, with these examples before them and
with last year's riots in Kobe and Osaka in mind, were
about to consider a new ordinance for the Metropolis.
Although there may have been other motives behind the
demonstrations, it seems certain that the primary motive
of the Communists was to force or frighten the Assembly
into adopting the kind of ordinance which would place
the least restrictions on their organizing activities.
As indicated above, those who instigated the
demonstration could not have known anything of the
proposed draft which was to be submitted to the Assembly,
since at the time of the demonstrations there were but
two copies of the draft in existence, and its contents
were apparently known only to a few SCAP officials. The
organizers certainly did not know that the proposed draft
followed the more lenient "model" prepared last year
by Government Section, and not the more restrictive version
which had already been adopted in some prefectures under
I Corps jurisdiction.
It is not known whether the draft ordinance referred
to above (apparently prepared by a SCAP Section, although
my informant did not say so) will be submitted in its
present form to the Tokyo Assembly, or whether the recent
demonstrations will result in the adoption of different
legislation. It is known, however, that some SCAP
officials consider the ordinances based on General Coulter's
suggestions to be unjustified restrictions of the right
of free assembly, and that these officials will not only
oppose the adoption of a similar ordinance in Tokyo,
but take action to have the ordinance already enacted
in other prefectures modified.
Comment:
The above information is based on a single. conversa-
tion, and none of the facts have been independently
confirmed. I have not seen a copy of General Coulter's
letter
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.96)
CONFIDENTIAL
to the military government teams, and it is posmibl
the interpretation of his instruotions contained in th
memorandum may be open to question. I have the highest
confidence in my informant's judgment and ability,
however, and I agree with him that, if his interpretation
is sound, ordinances based on General Coulter's suggest
constitute an unjustified abridgement of the right of fr
assembly as guaranteed in the Japanese Constitution.
Richard  fomb
June 5, 1949

POLITICAL SITUATION IN JAPAN
A. Introdaetion
The decision to accept the Allied surrender conditions
without qualification was taken by the Emperor on his oum
responsibility (heretofore unheard of in modern Japanose
history) on August 14, 1945 following disagreement among
his ministers and advisers. At that time he is reported to
have announced to his dissident ministere that thereafter
Japan would be "entirely separeted from the meens to wage
war end would be without eny arme or armement and thet Japan
would in this way contribute to world peace as a country
enjoying peace not maintained by erme". The Emperor also
ruled th t the utimate form of government in the new Japan
must depend upon the freely expreesed will of the Japanose
peorle.
In keeping with the foregoing prinei les, upon surrender,
Japen wos completely demilitorized, the Gront Jepan Political
Association dissolved, the secret oolice sbolished, meny
politic-l le dera urged from public life, and universal
suf rege introduced in preperetion for the virst post-war
general elections in pril 1946.
Meenvhile ScAl hed recuired
that the Je nese undertake to fulfill the rotcdem roclama-
tion by liberalizing their constitution. The new constitution
was drafted by a representetive group of Jananese under close
Hesüct arters direction and anproved by SCAr on March 5, 1946.

forces. The Emperor's role was reduced to that of a con-
stitutional monarch.
The first elections, conduoted under oareful Allied
supervision, brought into power a conservative coalition

ECONOMIC SITUATION IN JAPAN
Ae
Basic Economic Position
Japan's basic economic position is that of a densely
populeted 1sland manufacturing nation poor in natural
resouroes and dependent on foreign trade for its liveli-
hood. With virtually complete utillzation of available
land resources, Japan se agriculture was able to provide
only 80 per cent of the nation's food requirements in pre-
war yeers when the popul tion w s about 70 milllon. Today
it is so million and growing at a rate of over 1.5 million
a year, further increasing the country's dependence on
foreign trade. Lecking in virtually all the important
industrial rew materials except ordinary coal, Japen's
major sources of econonic etrength are a considerable
industrial plent, e lerge,  arü-working, and technically
competent workint cless, ond  capacity for industrial
organization.
