TOPSECRET
GENERAL NATURE OF THE PÉAGE ÉETTLENENT
GOVERNNENT DESIRES TO
The peace settlement for Japan should contain provisiems
for the termination of the war and the settlement of problems
directly arising therefrom and also for the establishment of
a basis for future relations of cooperation and peace between
Japan, as a member of the family of nations, and other powers.
A summary of the main matters which from this Government's
point of view should be included within the settlement is
given below. US policy on certain of these matters cannot be
established until after the views of the NME and the NSC-as
requested on May 23--have been received and dieeussed. US
policy on certain questions also will be affected by political
conditions as they develop prior to the time of the settlement.
I. Rights and Interests Relating to Property.
Provision should be made for the settlement of such ques-
tions as restitution of or compensation for property belonging
to members of the UN or UN nationals which was damaged or
destroyed in Japan, status of pre-war contracts between enemy
nationals, periods of prescription or limitation of right of
action involving enemy nationals, status of negotiable instru-
ments made before the war, industrial, literary and artistic
property, prize courts and judgments, etc. In so far as
these questions are or may be covered by FEC decisions the
principles of those decisions should be accepted and confirmed
by Japan in the peace settlement. For matters not so covered,
provisions along the general lines of articles dealing with
similar matters in the Italian treaty would be acceptable
(Articles 78, 79, 80, 81 and relevant annexes). These prin-
ciples might be included in the peace settlement or Japan
might merely accept an obligation to extend most-favored-nation
treatment on these matters to all members of the United Nations
leaving the details to be worked out on a bipartite basis.
II. Reparations.
TOP SECRET
SECRET

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SECRET
-2-
In the opinion of the US Government, the transfer of
further reparations from Japan would jeoparidse the implementa-
tion of the Japanese stabilization program on which the
successful accomplishment of the Allied occupation objectives
The
in Japan depends. In line with this pesition this Government
should accordingly be that the peace settlement should include
no provision for further reparations removals from Japan.
III. Level of Industry.
The US Government is of the opinion that support of the
principle that Japan should not be permitted to recover capacity
to make war does not require that Japan's production for peace-
ful purposes be limited or that limitations be imposed on
Japan's productive capacity in industries devoted to peaceful
purposes. This Government has regarded the establishment of
a level of industry for Japan solely as a means for obtaining
reparations from Japan. As this Government now considers that
urd
no further reparations should or can reasonably be exacted
from Japan, the position of this Government should be that no
provisions for the establishment or maintenance of a level of
industry should be included in the peace settlement.
IV. Territorial Settlements.
A. Southern Sakhalin. The Yalta Agreement provided
that the US, UK and the USSR had agreed that "the southern
part of Sakhalin as well as all the 1slands adjacent to
it shall be returned to the Soviet Union." Provision
should be made for the cession by Japan of Southern
Sakhalin and adjacent islands to the USSR.
B. The Kurils. The Yalta Agreement provided that
the US, UK and the USSR had agreed that "the Kuril Islands
shall be handed over to the Soviet Union." Provision
should be made for the transfer of the Kuril Islands to
the USSR. Two questions, however, remain to be settled:
SECRET TOP SECRET

=== Page 465 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
-3-
1. Whether an effort should be made to transfer
only the Kuril Islands which were ceded by Russim
to Japan by the Treaty of 1875 and to have Japan
retain the southernmost Kurils; and
2. Whether the transfer of the Kurils should be
in terms of outright cession or of administration
by the USSR under a UN trusteeship (strategic or
non-strategic).
C. The Bonin, Volcano and Marcus Islands. The
Potsdam Declaration provides that "Japanese sovereignty
shall be limited to the islands of Honshu, Hokkaido,
Hyushu, Shikoku and such minor islands as we determine."
Provision should be made that the US retain control over
the Bonin, Volcano and Marcus Islands which are now under
US military occupation. The question remains to be
settled, however, whether we should offer to administer
these islands under a UN trusteeship (strategic or non-
strategic) in accordance with President Truman's statement
of general policy toward Japanese islands on November 6,
1946, or should seek outright cession of the islands.
D. The Ryukyus. NSC 13 provides that it is the
intention of this Government to retain on a long-term
basis the facilities at Okinawa and such other facilities
as are deemed by the JCS to be necessary in the Ryukyu
Islands South of 29oN. A question remains to be settled
whether we should seek to obtain these facilities by
asking for bases in the Ryukyus and if so which bases, or
by offering to administer the islands under a UN trustee-
ship (strategic or non-strategic), or by asking for
their outright cession.
E. Formosa and the Pescadores. In the declaration
of the Cairo Conference it was stated to be the purpose
SECRET TOP SECRET

=== Page 466 === (confidence 1.00)
TOP SECRET
of the Three Great Allies that "all the territeries
Japan has stolen from the Chinese, such as Manehuria,
Formosa and the Pescadores, shall be restored to the
Republic of China." Formosa and the Pescadores have
been under the administrative control of China. Final
legal disposition of the island, however, awaits the
peace settlement. A decision remains to be taken con-
cerning the attitude which this Government should take
under present circumstances toward the whole question
of the future disposition of Formosa and the Pescadores.
Possible solutions include provision for outright cession
to China, provision for Chinese administration under a
UN trusteeship (strategic or non-strategic), or indepen-
dence with or without arrangements for US bases
F. Renunciation of Japanese Rights or Titles to
Korea. Provision should be made for the renunciation
by Japan of all rights and titles to Korea and adjacent
1slands. Korea has asserted claims)to Tsushima in Korea
strait--which this Government has regarded as Japanese--
but it is understood that these claim will not be
strongly pressed in connection with the settlement.
G. Renunciation of Japanese Rights or Titles to
the Trust Territory of the Pacific. Provision should
be made for the renunciation by Japan of all rights and
titles to the Trust Territory of the Pacific which is
administered by the US.
H. Renunciation of Japanese Rights or Titles to
Other Areas. Provision should be made for the renuncia-
tion by Japan of all rights and titles to Pratas (Chinese)
Hainan (Chinese), the Paracels (claimed by China and
France), the Spratlys (claimed by France and Japan), and
all other islands in the South China Sea as well as
all rights and titles to the Antarctic.
TOP SECRET
SECRET

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
-5-
I. Adjustment of Questions Arising from Territo
Cession. In connection with the provisions for the ees-
sion of territory by Japan to other powers as indtlated
above, customary arrangements should be included in the
settlement for adjustment of resulting nationality,
economic, financial and property questions relating to
the ceded territories.
V. Controls.
Proposals for extensive and continuing political, economic
and military controls over Japan are to be expected from
various FEC countries. For political controls, proposals may
include commitments by Japan to tm adhere permanently to a
specific and detailed reform program, including maintenance
of the present form of government, details of the judicial and
electoral systems, etc. For economic controls, proposals may
include provision for rigid limitations upon Japanese productive
capacity in a wide range of commodities including textiles, silk,
machinery, and over shipbuilding facilities, aviation, etc.
For military controls, proposals may include provision for
Japan's permanent demilitarization, including a permanent pro-
hibition against maintenance of land, sea or air forces,
possession or acquisition of any weapons except those required
for reasons of internal order, and a long-term agreement con-
templating Allied inspection and possibly supervision of Japan.
At present, the thinking in FE is along less extreme
lines.
On the political side, it is contemplated that Japan
should give an undertaking in general terms to continue along
the forward-looking path that it has been following since the
surrender. Specific reference would be included to enjoyment
of fundamental rights and freedoms (as in Article 15 of the
Italian Treaty).
On the economic side, it is contemplated that Japan would
TOP SECRET

=== Page 468 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRE
undertake to grant to each of the United Nations,
of reciprocity, most-favored-nation or national or mon-
discriminatory treatment with regard to commerce, industry,
shipping, duties and charges on imported goods, etc., along
the lines of Article 82 of the Italian Treaty. This undertaking
would be effective pending the conclusion of bipartite arrange-
ments between Japan and members of the UN which Japan would
agree to seek to negotiate within a specified period. Japan
would also accept an obligation to observe the provisions of
international agreements relating to such matters as fisheries,
narcotics, telecommunications, postal facilities together with
relevant regulations. A question would remain to be settled
concerning this Government's position on the extent of Japanese
participation in civil aviation and concerning the rights of
other powers with regard to aviation within and through Japan.
Finally, Japan should accept a prohibition on the stockpiling
of strategic raw materials in access of normal requirements
for consumption.
On the military and security side, the problem is com-
policated by the fact that Japan has assumed a pacifist status
under its Constitution and the UN to date has failed to work
out arrangements for security forces under Article 45 of the
Charter. Solutions now being tentatively considered include
( (a) continuance of the occupation in modified form;
(b) establishment of US bases on the main islands of
Japan, or outside the main islands (as e.g. in the
Ryukyus), for a limited period;
(c) a pact of some sort along the lines of the
Atlantic Pact.
Such measures--though cast in the form of defensive pro-
visions for Japan's benefit--would also perform the function
of ensuring observance of the treaty provisions for an initial
period. Heed might also be found, however-depending on the

specific measure for providing security that is chosen--fer
provisions for an inspection body of some sort such as
(a) a UN Commission for Japan;
(b) a supervisory body of Allied Powers--such as
envisaged in the draft disarmament and demilitariza-
tion treaty for Japan which was proposed by this
Government in 1946.
Depending on the solution adopted, provision might also
have to be made for the assumption by Japan of functions now
performed by SCAP and for the withdrawal of occupation forces.
VI. Miscellaneous Provisions Relating to the Termination
of the War.
The settlement should include provision concerning the
following matters:
1. Return of all prisoners of war and civilian internees,
to be completed within a specified time;
2. Renunciation by Japan of extraterritorial and
related rights in China;
3. Anundertaking on the part of Japan to ensure the
completion of smntences now being served by war criminals
in Japan;
4. Recognition by Japan of the full force of the peace
settlements made between the United Nations and other
belligerents;
5. An undertaking on the part of Japan to assist in
preventing the rearmament of Germany and Italy;
6. Revival or termination of bipartite and multipartite
treaties and agreements;
7. Provisions for the settlement of disputes arising
in connection with the peace settlement.
VII. Reestablishment of Diplomatic and Consular Relations.
consular relations should be made . Such provision might

form part of a general peace treaty or, together with the
items immediately following, it might be included in a
separate treaty dealing with the basis for future relations
between the Allied Powers and Japan.
VIII. Support for Japan's Admission to the UN.
The Allied Powers should undertake to support Japan's
application for admission to the UN. Japan for its part
should undertake to apply for admission in the UN and to
abide by the principles of the UN Charter.
IX. Statement of General Principles to Which All
Parties Subscribe.
